The Bayes Principle and Segal Axioms for P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} , with application to Periodic Covers
Jiasheng Lin

Abstract
The goal of the present paper is to reconcile the so-called P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} model from classical constructive quantum field theory (CQFT) with (metric version of) geometric and categorical axioms proposed by G. Segal [ 67 ] in the 90’s, in showing that the P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} model satisfies these axioms, appropriately adjusted. The approach is based on previous works of Dimock [ 17 ] and Pickrell [ 53 ] , while we give a new proof to a key step using what we call “the Bayes principle” of conditional probabilities in the infinite dimensional setting. We also give a precise statement and full proof of the locality of the P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} interaction. After that, we note that the so-called “transfer operator” associated to a cobordism has strictly positive kernel, this implies the operator has a spectral gap and allows to define a P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} Gibbs state on non-compact periodic curved surfaces (namely, with discrete “time translations”) by analogy with 1-dimensional spin chains. This also implies asymptotic properties of the P ​ ( ϕ ) 2 P(\phi)_{2} partition function on periodic covers of large degrees.



Introduction

In the classical approach to quantum field theory (QFT),
a central role is given to the representation of the Lorentz group as one can easily recognize if one looks at the Wightman axioms which were among the
first attempt to axiomatize QFT.
It was later realized by Feynman, Symanzik
[
70
]
, Nelson
[
50
]
in the 60’s that one could describe most objects of QFT using the functional integral, in imaginary time, which started the Euclidean approach to QFT. This allows to recover many computations and predictions of interest in quantum field theory.

In the 80’s, motivated by works of Atiyah and Witten on the relation of QFT with geometry and topology, there was an attempt to give an axiomatic definition of QFT based on geometry that would capture the main properties of the
functional integral representation.
The two important notions at the foundation of QFT are the concept of
locality
and
unitarity
. The first notion of locality is intuitively captured by the functional integral representation whereas the second notion will be encoded in certain symmetries of our manifolds under reflection called reflection positivity. The notion of reflection posivity plays a central role in constructive quantum field theory since the works of Osterwalder and Schrader, Glimm and Jaffe and also plays an important role in statistical physics.

The geometric axioms for QFT are due to Atiyah, Segal
[
67
]
and more recently Kontsevich and Segal
[
41
]
: instead of viewing QFT as representation of some space-time symmetry group, we rather view them loosely as linear representations of some geometric bordism category.
In the following subsection we give
the example of the 1 dimensional spin chain which contains the key phenomenology of the QFT model we will describe in the present work. It can be understood as linear representations of some discrete geometric bordism category and we will also explain why our spin chain admits a unique Gibbs measure (in the sense of Ruelle
[
64
]
) and the shift map acting on the chain is exponentially mixing for the Gibbs measure.

Example of 1D Spin Chain and its Transfer Operator

Our example illustrates the gluing properties by the transfer operator for discrete spin systems.
Consider
\mathbb{Z}_{N}:=\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
as a
1
-dimensional chain of size
N
, consider the space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
now as the space of maps
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, namely a discrete
path space
. The lattice site is denoted by
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and call
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
a
spin configuration
, whose value at the site
i
reads
\sigma(i)\in\mathbb{R}
. We impose
periodic boundary condition
, which means
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma(1)
. Thus the chain could be considered as circular.
We are given an action functional on the configuration space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
that reads

\displaystyle S_{N}(\sigma)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}P(\sigma(i))

(1.1)

where
P
is a polynomial bounded from below, the interaction is nearest neighbour.
Given a configuration
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
S_{N}(\sigma)
may be thought of as its “energy” and the statistical behaviour of the system is described by the probability measure called
Gibbs measure
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu_{P(\sigma)}(\sigma)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(N)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma,\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{Z}(N)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma

(1.2)

called the
partition function
of the system.

Now we would like to express this partition function
\mathcal{Z}(N)
in terms of elementary building blocks.
The main idea is to slice the action functional as

\displaystyle S_{N}(\sigma)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big[|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(P(\sigma(i+1))+P(\sigma(i)))\Big]

so exponentiating gives

\displaystyle\exp\left(-S_{N}(\sigma)\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}K(\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i))

(1.3)

where

K(x,y)=\textrm{e}^{-|x-y|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(P(x)+P(y))}

(1.4)

which is the Schwartz kernel of an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that is smoothing.

Definition 1.1
.

Define the
transfer operator

T
to be exactly the operator with kernel
K(x,y)
, that is,

(TF)(x)=\int K(x,y)F(y)\mathrm{d}y,

(1.5)

for any function(al)
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Then we see immediately from (
1.3
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(N)=\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\big[K(\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i))\mathrm{d}\sigma(i)\big]=\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(T^{N}),

(1.6)

remembering that
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma(1)
, since for a smoothing operator
A:L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
we have
\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(A)=\int K_{A}(x,x)\mathrm{d}x
with
K_{A}
being the integral kernel.

More generally we would like to express the kernel of
T^{N}
in terms of
\exp(-S(\sigma))
using the relation (
1.3
). This means instead of letting the boundary condition be periodic we let
\sigma(1)=\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\sigma(N+1)=\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
given two boundary conditions
(\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then the kernel
K_{N}
of
T^{N}
is

\displaystyle K_{N}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})

\displaystyle=\int K(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma(N))\cdots K(\sigma(2),\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})\prod_{i=2}^{N}\mathrm{d}\sigma(i)

(1.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N-1}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})}\prod_{i=2}^{N}\mathrm{d}\sigma(i),

(1.8)

with the
conditioned
interaction
S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
defined as

S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}P(\sigma(i))+\frac{1}{2}(P(\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})+P(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})),

(1.9)

with
\sigma(1)\equiv\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
. Now if
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
are two integers and we define the kernels
K_{N_{1}}
,
K_{N_{2}}
using (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) with
N
replaced respectively by
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
, then it follows “trivially” from the composition property
T^{N_{2}}\circ T^{N_{1}}=T^{N_{2}+N_{1}}
that

K_{N_{2}+N_{1}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})=\int K_{N_{2}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma)K_{N_{1}}(\sigma,\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})\mathrm{d}\sigma.

(1.10)

However, such a relation becomes remarkable (rather than trivial) if we do not have the “unit” transfer operator
T
to start with; that is, if we do not have (
1.7
) but define
K_{N_{1}}
and
K_{N_{2}}

directly
with an expression of the form (
1.8
) and (
1.9
).
This corresponds to the idea of a
path integral
in quantum mechanics and quantum field theory. Alternatively one could consider the Gibbs measure (
1.2
) and take
K_{N}
as the
transition probability
of a certain stochastic process. Then (
1.10
) is the
Chapman-Kolmogorov
equation which relies heavily on the fact that the underlying process is
Markovian
. In a sense, for both interpretations a crucial condition is that the interaction
S(\sigma)
be
local
; that is, very roughly speaking, if one chops the sites
[1,N_{1}+N_{2}]:=\{1,2,\dots,N_{1}+N_{2}\}
into
[1,N_{1}]\sqcup[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]
then
S_{[1,N_{1}+N_{2}]}(\sigma)\approx S_{[1,N_{1}]}(\sigma|_{[1,N_{1}]})+S_{[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]}(\sigma|_{[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]})
.

The main result of this article concerns a 2-dimensional “continuum” version of this story where lattice sites are replaced by the continuum of points on a 2D surface (considered as space-time) and a configuration is replaced by a distribution. See the section below for a more precise description. In the final section, we also show that when the space-time admits a periodic translation symmetry then a more precise analogy with the spin chain described above can be restored, in particular, there exists a
Gibbs state
in the
thermodynamic limit
. Further discussion of the above example continues in the separate introduction to this latter part in section
7.2
.

Main results and Comments

Main results.

Let
\Omega
be a Riemannian surface whose boundary has two components
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
. We will define probability measures
\mu_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}
respectively on the spaces of distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
and put
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}),\mu_{\mathrm{in}})
,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}=L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}),\mu_{\mathrm{out}})
which play the role of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
above. We then define the “transfer operator” associated to
\Omega
to be given by the integral kernel which is formally

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}},\varphi_{\mathrm{out}})=\int_{\{\phi|\partial\Omega=(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}},\varphi_{\mathrm{out}})\}}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})+P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}(x)}[\mathcal{L}\phi],

(1.11)

where the formal integration is over the space of all distributions subject to the boundary condition
\phi|_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\phi|_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}
, with respect to the non-existent Lebesgue measure
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
on the space of such distributions. Here
P(\phi)
is a polynomial bounded below just like the one considered in (
1.1
). If
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
are two Riemannian surfaces of the kind as above where the “out” boundary of
\Omega_{1}
is isometric to the “in” boundary of
\Omega_{2}
via an isometry
\rho
, then one can glue them along
\rho
and obtain the surface
\Omega_{2}\cup_{\rho}\Omega_{1}
. This article then seeks to prove the analogue of (
1.7
) for operators defined by (
1.11
) in such a situation, after making rigorous sense of the formula which is in fact a large part of the work. The main development is carried out in section
6
and the proof is completed in subsection
6.5
.

After that, we point out that the operator
U_{\Omega}
defined by (
1.11
) has the
Perron-Frobenius
property, namely it has strictly positive kernel (this could already be seen from the above heuristic expression). This has the consequence that
U_{\Omega}
in fact has a
spectral gap
and together with the composition property obtained above implies that the
P(\phi)_{2}
theory on periodic space-times behaves very similarly to the spin-chain example discussed above. In particular, we are able to construct a
P(\phi)_{2}
Gibbs state on some periodic infinite volume surface in perfect analogy with the 1D spin chain, we refer to section
7.3
for further details.

Concrete illustration.

For a concrete illustration of our results, we state an informal theorem which is a consequence of the Segal gluing of
P(\phi)_{2}
amplitudes developed in the present work.

Theorem

(asymptotics of the partition function on large degree periodic covers)
.

Let
(M,g)
denotes a compact Riemannian surface,
M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M
a Riemannian
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of
M
and
\gamma
the generator of the deck group.
We can think of
M_{\infty}
as the infinite composition of some given cobordism
\Omega
, which serves as the “fundamental domain” of the deck transformations on
M_{\infty}
.
For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
M_{N}:=M_{\infty}/\gamma^{N}
the cyclic cover of degree
N
of
M
and
\Delta_{N}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M_{N}
.
We define the partition function of the
P(\phi)_{2}
theory on
M_{N}
heuristically as

Z_{N}\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M_{N})}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M_{N}}P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V(x)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{N}}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V}[\mathcal{L}\phi]

(1.12)

where
\mathrm{d}V
is the Riemannian area form,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M_{N})
is the space of real distributions on
M_{N}
and
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
is the non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M_{N})
.

Then the renormalized sequence of free energies

\frac{1}{N}\log\left(Z_{N}\right)
has a limit
\lambda_{0}
when
N\rightarrow+\infty
, moreover this limit
\lambda_{0}
can be interpreted as the leading eigenvalue of some transfer operator
U_{\Omega}
which is the quantization of the cobordism
\Omega
mentioned above.

Rigorous sense of the expression (
1.12
) will be made in sections
2
and
3
.

Remark 1.1
.

The above theorem is an interacting QFT version of a result of Naud on the asymptotics of zeta determinants on large degree random covers of compact surfaces
[
47
]
, the main difference is that he treats random covers whereas our sequence of covers is deterministic and he only deals with the partition function of free fields whereas we treat the interacting case.

Other parts.

In sections
2
and
3
we retrace the classical rigorous construction due to Nelson of the integrand of (
1.11
), and adapt the argument for showing
locality
of the interaction in subsection
5.5
. Section
4
aims to derive the behavior of a Gaussian field under the trace map (restriction to hypersurface); these results are classical but our treatment still gives a somewhat new perspective based on elementary adjoint relations of various geometric operators (see subsection
4.3
). Section
5
discusses the Markov property that culminates in the
Bayes principle
(see below) for the GFF and proving locality of the
P(\phi)
interaction. In between, we give comments on
reflection positivity
which is not strictly related to the aim of the article but, the author believes, integrates organically into the discussion (subsections
4.5
and
5.4
).

Comments.

Firstly we point out that the exact same problem has been treated some 15 years ago in Pickrell
[
53
]
, and a partial treatment (trace axiom for free field) also appears in Dimock
[
17
]
. The present article aims to offer a new proof (to a core proposition, see below) based on the following simple idea. We also tie up many loose ends around this problem; especially we precisely state and prove the
locality
of the
P(\phi)
interaction, which is widely believed to lie behind a property such as Segal’s gluing rule and, as we shall see in our case in section
5.5
, lies behind the very possibility of defining (
1.11
) over a domain with boundary. Indeed, since the definition of the
P(\phi)
interaction requires
renormalization
, the verification of Segal’s rules provides an important
testing ground
that the renormalization procedures can actually be made local, which is in general a deep question. See also remark
3.1
.

The idea (observation) is that Segal’s gluing axioms for the scalar QFT actually follow from the
Bayes principle
applied to the functional measure of the QFT. What we mean by the “Bayes principle” here is the following, in its simplest form: suppose two real random variables
X
and
Y
have joint probability law
\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)}
; then we have two equal expressions for their joint density
p(x,y)=(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)}/\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\otimes 2})(x,y)
, namely

p(x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\pi^{x}_{*}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)})}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{Y|X=x}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\pi^{y}_{*}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)})}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(y)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X|Y=y}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(x),

(1.13)

where
\pi^{x}
,
\pi^{y}
are respectively the projections onto the
x
- and
y
-axes,
\mathbb{P}_{Y|X=x}
denotes the conditional law of
Y
knowing “
X=x
”, and vice versa for
\mathbb{P}_{X|Y=y}
(more rigorously these are expressed using
transition kernels
). In other words, one could evaluate
p(x,y)
by
conditioning
in two alternative ways: on
X
or on
Y
. In what follows we put forward a version of (
1.13
) for the Gaussian Free Field (GFF) measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
for
M
a closed Riemannian surface, where the projections
\pi^{x,y}
are replaced by trace maps (restrictions) onto embedded Riemannian circles. By the spacial Markov property of the GFF, the expressions for the conditional laws simplify nicely. Since the transition amplitudes (
1.11
) are given by the square roots of the densities of the trace-induced measures with respect to fixed background Gaussian functional measures on the circles (corresponding to
p(x,y)
above), we obtain a proof of Segal’s gluing axioms for the GFF and, by the
locality
of the
P(\phi)
interaction, the result extends immediately to the interacting case for this interaction.

Presumably in general, if one has a
local
interaction such that the QFT Gibbs measure (
2.1
) is well-defined and absolutely continuous with respect to the free field measure, then Segal’s rules for the interacting theory hold based on that of the free theory. See, for example, Guillarmou, Kupiainen, Rhodes and Vargas
[
29
]
for the case of the Liouville theory (which is conformal). In Pickrell
[
53
]
, the argument for free field (presumably) used composition formulae in the
oscillator semigroup
1
1

1

this is a semigroup consisting of operators with
Gaussian kernels
, and the reader could see that (
1.11
) with
P=0
is analogous to such a kernel. The author is however not very familiar with this object and refers the reader to Howe
[
35
]
and Folland
[
23
]
chapter 5.
, and proceeded by analogy with the finite dimensional case (see Howe
[
35
]
). In
[
29
]
there is a counterpart of the treatment based on computations of Dirichlet forms. The proof here is different from both
[
29
]
and
[
53
]
, which is geometric and in fact conceptually transparent. More precisely, this is proposition
6.9
below which corresponds to proposition 3 on page 16 of
[
53
]
and (the second part of)
2
2

2

the first part corresponds to a combination of corollary
2.15
and proposition
4.9
of this article, the first of which is largely a known result concerning quadratic perturbation of Gaussian fields (see references in section
2.3
).
lemma 5.3 on page 30 of
[
29
]
.

An important ingredient taken from (inspired by) Pickrell
[
53
]
is the definition of the amplitude as the square root of the Radon-Nikodym density comparing a trace-induced measure to a fixed background Gaussian measure. This hints at an infinite-dimensional analogue of the notion of “half-densities”, and is actually what
[
53
]
considered. This latter notion has the advantage of being intrinsic. But due to insufficient literature discussing half-densities in the infinite-dimensional setting
3
3

3

D. Pickrell confirmed with the author that he did not know of a reference which discuss infinite-dimensional half-densities other than the appendix of
[
53
]
. The author agrees with him that this notion might be a helpful one worthy of a detailed development.
, we adopt here the equivalent approach of fixing a background measure. Indeed, there is only one correct amplitude to consider, prescribed by physics, and the reader would see that the expression we obtain from this definition agrees, for example, with the one of
[
29
]
in its form and exactly for the free field. In fact, this definition of the amplitude “derives” naturally from a special case of Segal’s trace axiom (see section
6.3
).

Remark 1.2
.

We consider
real
scalar fields, namely a field configuration
\phi
(on
M
) is a
real
distribution (on
M
). In yet other words, this means if we pair
\phi
with a potentially complex test function
f
, then

\overline{\phi(f)}=\phi(\overline{f}),

(1.14)

the bar denoting complex conjugation. Without loss of generality, all function spaces are assumed real unless otherwise stated.

Remark 1.3

(role of symmetries)
.

One primary aim of the present work is to reconcile the model (
2.1
) with the axiomatics proposed by G. Segal
[
67
]
. However, a significant difference between our model and the theories
[
67
]
principally tries to target is that we do not have and consider symmetries beyond the simplest one of reflecting across a hypersurface. In particular the model (
2.1
) will not be conformally invariant, and not even the free case (
P=0
) as
m>0
and we do not include any “conformal anomaly” accounting effects of a conformal change in the metric. A linear term involving the Gauss curvature could have been included in the exponential of (
1.11
) — our situation corresponds to the case of “minimal coupling”, see discussion of Jaffe and Ritter
[
38
]
around equation (7).

In fact, we lack a basic symmetry which is present in the Euclidean (
\mathbb{R}^{2}
) case of the same model (
2.1
): since we deal with general compact surfaces, there are no distinguished “time translations” (in the form of a generating Killing vector field). In the Euclidean case, the “canonical” time direction helps to fix a definitive Hilbert space of quantum states, and eventually allows one to obtain an explicit expression of the Hamiltonian operator generating time evolution of the theory. This would also be the case if
\Omega
is a cylinder equipped with cylindrical metric, for example, but for general
\Omega
the notion of a “Hamiltonian operator” is not very well-defined. It is nevertheless interesting to think of a definition perhaps in terms of categorical limits as in Kontsevich and Segal
[
41
]
section 3. Basic constructions of a Euclidean (Riemannian) field theory on curved space-times equipped with a time translation have been considered in Jaffe and Ritter
[
39
]
.

Last but not least, in the last section
7
of this article we explore a crucial property of the operator
U_{\Omega}
representing the “evolution” across the cobordism
\Omega
(more suggestively it could be written as
\textrm{e}^{-[\Omega]H}
), namely that it is
Perron-Frobenius
. This would imply that it nevertheless has many properties in common with an actual time evolution (i.e.
\textrm{e}^{-tH}
), especially it has a
spectral gap
.

Remark 1.4
.

In hindsight, the author feels that a proof following (
1.13
) is a natural one regarding the intuition behind the path integral as “summing over histories”, and (
1.13
) simply says that such a sum ‘‘conditioned’’ on different intermediate points in history
4
4

4

In our case, history is circular!
produce equivalent results (the proof of (
1.13
) itself is trivial!). However, a still more ideal version of the proof would perhaps be one that puts the free field and interacting field on equal footing, where Segal’s axioms follow from a generalized version of “Nelson’s axioms” as explained on section IV.1 of Simon
[
72
]
, based on the Markov property and uses a “nonlinear version” of the transition operator
\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}
(see section
5.3
).

Notations

In this paper, unless stated otherwise,

“
\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}
” means “heuristically equal to”;

(M,g)=
closed Riemannian surface with metric
g
;

(\Omega,g)=
compact Riemannian surface with metric
g
, boundary
\partial\Omega
, seen as isometrically embedded in
M
, the induced metric on
\partial\Omega
is still denoted
g
;

\Sigma=
disjoint union of Riemannian circles (of different radii), usually
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
; hypersurface in
M
;

\Omega^{\circ}=
interior of
\Omega
;

m=
mass parameter,
m>0
,
fixed throughout paper
;

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})=

real
distributions on
M
and
\Omega
;

C^{\infty}(M)
,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})=
smooth functions on
M
, smooth compact support functions on
\Omega^{\circ}
;

\Delta=
Laplacian on
(M,g)
,
\Delta f:=-\ddiv(\nabla f)
, therefore it is defined to be
nonnegative
, the same applies below;

\Delta_{\Sigma}=
Laplacian on
\Sigma
;

\Delta_{\Omega,D}=
Laplacian on
\Omega
with (zero) Dirichlet boundary condition (see also remark
4.1
);

\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}=(\Delta_{\Sigma}+m^{2})^{1/2}
;

\tau_{\Sigma}=
trace operator (restriction) onto the hypersurface
\Sigma
;

(Q,\mathcal{O})=
general probability sample space with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{O}
;

C=
general positive self-adjoint elliptic pseudodifferential operator on
M
,
\Omega
,
\Sigma
, which is Hilbert-Schmidt on the corresponding
L^{2}
spaces;

\mu_{C}^{\mathrm{cond}}=\mu_{C^{-1}}^{\mathrm{cond}}=
Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
, or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
, equipped with their Fréchet Borel
\sigma
-algebra, with covariance
\mathbb{E}_{C}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,Ch\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, under some conditions;

\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}=
massive GFF measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
;
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}=
massive Dirichlet GFF measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
;

\mathbb{E}_{B}^{A}=
expectation under
\mu_{B}^{A}
;

\phi(f)=
the random variable
\phi\mapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
indexed by
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
or
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
;

W^{s}(M)=
the
L^{2}
Sobolev space on
M
, with inner product
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{W^{s}(M)}:=\left\langle-,(\Delta+m^{2})^{s}-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
;

W^{s}_{A}(M)
,
W^{s}_{U}(M)
,
W^{s}(U)=
see appendix
C.1
;

\Psi^{r}(M)
,
\Psi^{r}(\Sigma)=
pseudodifferential operators (
\Psi
DOs) on
M
or
\Sigma
with order
r
;

[\mathcal{L}\phi]=
hypothetical Lebesgue measure on a space of distributions;

\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=
trace norm, Hilbert-Schmidt norm,
\mathcal{J}_{p}
ideal norm (see appendix
A.1
), operator norm acting on
L^{2}
or
L^{2}
-norm on function;

\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}=
Poisson integral operator extending from
\Sigma
to
\Omega
, with boundary conditions
B
imposed on boundary components
other than

\Sigma
(see also remark
4.1
);

\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}=
Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator or the jumpy version defined using
\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}
(jumpy version is understood when
\Sigma
is in the interior of
\Omega
rather than a boundary component);

\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega,B}=
Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
(\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B})^{-1}
;

\mu_{2\DN}^{\partial\Omega,\Omega}=
Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega)
with covariance
\frac{1}{2}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}
;

\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}=
Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-1}
;

\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}=\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}
is the
transition operator
defined in section
5.3
.

Gaussian Fields on a Riemannian Manifold

In this and the next section
we define rigorously a probability measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
, as well as certain variants on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
, which heuristically bears the form

\mathrm{d}\mu_{P}(\phi)\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\frac{1}{Z}\overbrace{\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}}^{A}\underbrace{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]}_{B},

(2.1)

where
P
is a polynomial bounded from below, such as
P(\phi)=\phi^{4}-\phi^{2}
,
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
denotes the nonexistent Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and

Z_{M}\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]

(2.2)

is the normalization factor, also called the
partition function
.

The idea is that while
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
is nonexistent, together with the factor
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M})
the expression
B
can be given a rigorous meaning as a Gaussian probability measure scaled by a (finite) volume constant, and the measure (
2.1
) can be constructed if, after defining part
A
rigorously, one proves that it is
L^{1}
with respect to the measure
B
. This construction of (
2.1
) was done on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
essentially by Edward Nelson
[
49
]
and the argument is already a classic, very well-understood (see Simon
[
72
]
, Glimm and Jaffe
[
27
]
, Dimock
[
18
]
chapter 13 or Hairer
[
32
]
section 9). We will build on this method.

For us, the argument must be carefully adapted to show
locality
of the
P(\phi)
interaction, in the curved surface setting (see remark
3.1
). Leaving details to section
3
, we stress here the main theme of the subject: since powers of a distribution is generally not defined,
renormalization
is needed, and the polynomial
P(\phi)
must in fact be replaced by the so-called
Wick-ordered
polynomial
{:}P(\phi){:}
for
A
to exist.

In this section
we construct the measure
B
. This construction is a classical one in probability theory, usually called the
Gaussian free field
. Here we collect some essential concepts and results, and refer, for example, to Sheffield
[
68
]
and Powell and Werner
[
55
]
for more information.

The starting point is that for a real positive symmetric
N\times N
matrix
C^{-1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{x},C^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}^{N}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{(2\pi)^{N/2}}{(\det C^{-1})^{1/2}}

(2.3)

and the measure

(\det C^{-1})^{1/2}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{x},C^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}

(2.4)

is a Gaussian measure with covariance matrix
C
(in the standard basis), where we absorb
(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
into the Lebesgue measure. Thus we should define

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\textrm{``}\det\textrm{''}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}(\phi)

(2.5)

where
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is a Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
with covariance
operator

(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
, and “
\det
” is an infinite dimensional generalization of the determinant of a matrix, called the
\zeta
-regularized determinant
. The Gaussian measure and the determinant are two issues to be treated separately (in sections
2.1
and
2.2
).

The Massive Gaussian Free Field

Definition and Representations

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold
of dimension
d
with metric
g
, and
\Omega\subset M
an open domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
, both equipped with the metric induced from
g
(same notation). Fix
m>0
as the mass parameter.

Definition 2.1
.

The
massive Gaussian Free Field (GFF)
with mass
m
on
M
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C^{\infty}(M)
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.6)

for any
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

Definition 2.2
.

The
Dirichlet massive Gaussian Free Field
with mass
m
on
\Omega
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\big\langle f,(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big\langle P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}f,P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}h\big\rangle_{W^{-1}},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.7)

for any
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. See appendix
C.1
and in particular lemma
C.4
.

Remark 2.1
.

There is also a technical requirement: the family of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
(respectively,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
) should generate the
\sigma
-algebra of the underlying sample space. This makes the corresponding random processes
full
, per the probabilitic terminology. We will take this requirement for granted in the sequel.

We have chosen to start with these rather abstract definitions due to the fact that there are many choices of sample spaces (
Q
-spaces) on which to realize those Gaussian processes. These realizations are all equivalent in the sense explained in appendix
B.2
. For the sake of concreteness, we point out that one choice for the sample space is
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
(or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
).

Proposition 2.1

(Bochner-Minlos,
[
12
]
theorem 5.11, page 266)
.

There exists a Borel probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
on the Fréchet space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
such that
\phi\mapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=:\phi(f)
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, realizes the random variable
\phi(f)
of the massive GFF on
M
.

Similarly, there exists a Borel probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
on the Fréchet space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
such that
\phi\mapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
,
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, realizes the random variable
\phi(f)
of the Dirichlet massive GFF on
\Omega
.

Remark for proof.

The key point is that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
(respectively,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
) is the dual of a nuclear space
C^{\infty}(M)
(respectively,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
). There are other approaches, see remark
2.2
below and appendix
B.2
.
∎

Notation
.

Denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
the expectations under
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
respectively.

Remark 2.2
.

Based on the spectral theory of
\Delta
, let
\left\{\varphi_{j}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
be its complete orthonormal eigenfunctions with (real nonnegative) eigenvalues
\left\{\lambda_{j}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
,
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda_{2}\leqslant\dots\leqslant\lambda_{j}\leqslant\cdots
, counted with multiplicity. Then the
GFF with mass
m
on
M
could also be represented as the random formal series

\phi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\xi_{j}\varphi_{j}

(2.8)

where the sequence
(\xi_{j})
has the law

\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}(0,(\lambda_{j}+m^{2})^{-1}),

(2.9)

where
\mathcal{N}(0,\lambda)
is the standard centered Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
with variance
\lambda
. Similarly, the Dirichlet GFF on
\Omega
could also be so represented using eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{\Omega,D}
, which are complete for
L^{2}(\Omega)
. See also appendix
B.2
.

We also consider a slightly more general situation where
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
is replaced by an operator
C
.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a bounded self-adjoint positive elliptic pseudodifferential operator
C
of order
-s
on
M
(
s>0
) is a
Gaussian covariance operator
of order
-s
.

The same results go through (proposition
2.1
and remark
2.2
), and one obtains a measure
\mu_{C}
on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
.

Definition 2.4
.

The
Gaussian Field
on
M
with
covariance operator

C
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C^{\infty}(M)
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,Ch\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.10)

for any
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
. We denote the corresponding measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
by
\mu_{C}
and the expectation with respect to this measure by
\mathbb{E}_{C}
.

Remark 2.3
.

For
C
satisfying the assumptions, the inner product
(f,h)\mapsto\left\langle f,Ch\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
defines an equivalent norm for
W^{-s}(M)
.

Essential Properties

It follows directly from the definitions that

Lemma 2.2
.

The
Gaussian Hilbert space
of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is
W^{-1}(M)
, and that of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
is
W^{-1}(\Omega)
.
\Box

From (iii) of lemma
B.3
and (iii) of lemma
C.3
,

Lemma 2.3
.

The
Cameron-Martin space
of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is
W^{1}(M)
, and that of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
is
W^{1}_{\Omega}(M)
.
\Box

Remark 2.4
.

There is a tacit assumption in the way we defined our fields: we expected the random variable
\phi(f)
to come from the distributional pairing (
L^{2}
-pairing) between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M)
(respectively,
\Omega
). Other pairings may also be used. For example, let
\phi(f)
be
\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{W^{1}(M)}
instead of
\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
. One then needs to alter the covariances accordingly. Indeed, they are related by

\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\phi,f\big\rangle_{W^{1}}\big\langle\phi,h\big\rangle_{W^{1}}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})h\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big]=\big\langle f,h\big\rangle_{W^{1}(M)}.

(2.11)

This way of definition is noticeably used by Sheffield
[
68
]
. The Gaussian Hilbert space in this case is
W^{1}(M)
(respectively,
W^{1}_{\Omega}(M)
). Since we are nevertheless defining the same measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
, the Cameron-Martin spaces are the same. This pairing is more natural in view of the
abstract Wiener space
construction of the
Q
-space of a Gaussian process indexed by a Hilbert space (now both the indexing and the pairing are provided by
W^{1}
). See appendix
B.2
, and also remark
C.4
.

Similarly,

Lemma 2.4
.

The Gaussian Hilbert space of
\mu_{C}
is
W^{-s}(M)
, equipped with
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, and the Cameron-Martin space is
W^{s}(M)
, equipped with
\langle-,C^{-1}-\rangle_{L^{2}}
.
\Box

Corollary 2.5

(cf. proposition
B.2
)
.

Let
\phi_{0}\in W^{s}(M)
and denote by
(\phi_{0})_{*}\mu_{C}
the measure image of
\mu_{C}
under the shift
\phi\mapsto\phi+\phi_{0}
. Then the Radon-Nikodym density between
(\phi_{0})_{*}\mu_{C}
and
\mu_{C}
is

\frac{\mathrm{d}((\phi_{0})_{*}\mu_{C})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}}(\phi)=\textrm{e}^{\phi(C^{-1}\phi_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{0},C^{-1}\phi_{0}\rangle_{L^{2}}}.\quad\Box

(2.12)

Finally we say about the supports of the measures in the closed manifold case.

Lemma 2.6
.

We have
\mu_{C}(W^{-\delta}(M))=1
for any
\delta>\frac{1}{2}(d-s)
.

Proof.

We rely on lemma
2.17
. Pick
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that the real power
C^{-2t/s}
is a positive elliptic
\Psi
DO of order
2t
, and hence
\langle-,C^{-2t/s}-\rangle_{L^{2}}
gives an equivalent representation of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{W^{t}(M)}^{2}
. Thus whenever
C^{-\frac{2t}{s}+1}
is trace class,

\mathbb{E}_{C}\big[\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{W^{t}(M)}^{2}\big]\approx\mathbb{E}_{C}\big[\big\langle\phi,C^{-2t/s}\phi\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big]=\ttr_{L^{2}}\big(C^{-\frac{2t}{s}+1}\big)<\infty,

(2.13)

and it then follows
\mu_{C}(W^{t}(M))=1
. Since now
C^{-\frac{2t}{s}+1}
is a
\Psi
DO of order
2t-s
, it is trace class when
2t-s<-d
namely
t<-\frac{1}{2}(d-s)
, finishing the proof.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

We point out that
\mu_{C}(W^{-\delta}(M))=1
for any
\delta>0
in the following two cases:

1.

\dim M=2
and
\mu_{C}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
;

2.

\dim M=1
and
C
has order
-1
.

Last but not least, we make the following innocent but useful observation.

Lemma 2.7
.

If
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
(possibility of non-empty boundary in either or both components), then GFFs (indeed, Gaussian fields) over
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are independent and
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,B}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega_{1},B}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega_{2},B}
where
B=D
when the corresponding
\Omega
,
\Omega_{1}
or
\Omega_{2}
has boundary and
B=\varnothing
when either is closed.
\Box

Determinants

In this section we discuss (two) generalizations of the notion of the determinant (of a matrix) to infinite dimensional operators. Their starting points are as follows. Suppose
A
is a matrix (acting on
V
) with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
, …,
\lambda_{N}
counted with algebraic multiplicity. Firstly, one has the equality

\det A=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}=\exp\left(-\partial_{z}(\lambda_{1}^{-z}+\cdots+\lambda_{N}^{-z})(0)\right)=\exp\left(-\partial_{z}\ttr(A^{-z})(0)\right),

(2.14)

which suggests defining
\det A
, when
A
acts on infinite dimensions, as
\exp\left(-\partial_{z}\ttr(A^{-z})(0)\right)
when both
A^{-z}
(as a function of
z\in\mathbb{C}
) and the derivative of its trace (at
z=0
) makes sense; this is called the
zeta-regularized determinant
. Secondly, if
\mathbbm{1}
is the identity, one also has

\det(\mathbbm{1}+A)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}(1+\lambda_{j})=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\lambda_{i_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{i_{k}}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\ttr_{\Lambda^{k}V}(\Lambda^{k}A),

(2.15)

where
\Lambda^{k}A
is the
k
-th
exterior/antisymmetric product
of
A
, acting on the exterior product space
\Lambda^{k}V
. This suggests defining

\det(\mathbbm{1}+A)\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\ttr(\Lambda^{k}A)

(2.16)

in infinite dimensions when all of
\Lambda^{k}A
are
trace class
and the infinite sum converges, and is called the
Fredholm determinant
.

General references include Kontsevich and Vishik
[
42
]
, Shubin
[
69
]
sections 9-13, Simon
[
71
]
chapter 3 and finally Gohberg, Goldberg and Krupnik
[
26
]
. See also Dang
[
16
]
for a quick acquaintance of the physical-geometric context and Quine, Heydari and Song
[
56
]
for an interesting discussion of zeta-regularization of infinite products.

Zeta-regularized and Fredholm Determinants

The zeta-regularized determinant was first introduced by Ray and Singer
[
57
]
. The first step is to define the
zeta function
of a (rather special) pseudodifferential operator
A
over a manifold
M
with or without boundary,

\zeta_{A}(z)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\ttr_{L^{2}}(A^{-z})

(2.17)

as a function of the complex variable
z
and study its meromorphic extension over a region that includes
z=0
. For our purposes
A
is
\Delta+m^{2}
,
(\Delta+m^{2})^{1/2}
or a Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. These are positive elliptic
\Psi
DOs of positive order such that the principal symbol
\sigma_{A}(x,\xi)
is
strictly positive
whenever
\xi\neq 0
. In particular their spectra are in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and does not intersect
\mathbb{B}_{\rho}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}
for some
\rho>0
. This enables one to define the complex power
A^{-z}
using the Cauchy integral representation

A^{-z}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma}\textrm{e}^{-z\log\lambda}(A-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda,

(2.18)

where
\gamma
is the contour (with parametrization traversing in order)

\gamma=\{r\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi}\penalty\ |\penalty\ r>\rho\}\cup\{\rho\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\penalty\ |\penalty\ -\pi<\theta<\pi\}\cup\{r\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}\penalty\ |\penalty\ r>\rho\},

(2.19)

and
\log\lambda
taken to be the principal branch defined on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
with
\log 1=0
.

Proposition 2.8

(
[
69
]
proposition 10.1, theorems 10.1, 13.1, 13.2, also
[
66
]
)
.

We have

1.

For the operators
A
under consideration, we have the bound

\left\lVert(A-\lambda)^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant c|\lambda|^{-1}

(2.20)

for
\lambda\in\gamma
, and the integral (
2.18
) defines
A^{-z}
as a holomorphic function valued in bounded
L^{2}
operators, for
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>0
. It continues as such a holomorphic operator function to all
z\in\mathbb{C}
via

A^{-z}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}A^{k}A^{-z-k},

(2.21)

where
k
is any integer with
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>-k
so that
A^{-z-k}
is defined by (
2.18
), and the definition (
2.21
) does not depend on
k
.

2.

If
A
has order
s
then
A^{-z}
defined as above is a classical
\Psi
DO of order
-zs
. In particular it is trace class on
L^{2}(M)
when
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>d/s
,
d=\dim M
, for which
\zeta_{A}(z)
is well-defined by (
2.17
). Moreover, for these
z
,

\zeta_{A}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-z},

(2.22)

where
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are the eigenvalues of
A
, and the sum converges absolutely, and uniformly in
z
over
\{\mathfrak{Re}(z)>d/s+\varepsilon\}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

3.

Finally,
\zeta_{A}(z)
can be meromorphically continued over
\mathbb{C}
with simple poles possible at
\{\frac{d}{s},\frac{d-1}{s},\frac{d-2}{s},\cdots\}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}
, and holomorphic elsewhere. In particular, it is holomorphic at
z=0
.
\Box

Definition 2.5
.

For an operator
A
under consideration, we define its
zeta-regularized determinant
as

\detz A\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp\left(-\partial_{z}\zeta_{A}(0)\right),

(2.23)

where
\zeta_{A}(0)
is the
zeta function
of
A
given by (
2.17
) and (
2.18
).

Remark 2.6
.

An alternative way of defining the zeta function and its meromorphic continuation is to use the heat kernel and the Mellin transform. See Gilkey
[
25
]
section 1.12. This way of definition also gives (
2.22
) over the same region, defining therefore the same function as ours.

Remark 2.7
.

From (ii) of proposition
2.8
we see that if
A
is self-adjoint and strictly positive, then
\zeta_{A}(z)
is real-valued for
z\in(d/s,+\infty)
. But
\mathfrak{Im}(\zeta_{A})
is real analytic and hence
\zeta_{A}
remains real-valued on
\mathbb{R}
before crossing a pole, and by (iii) it is in particular real-valued on an interval around
0
. Thus
\partial_{z}\zeta_{A}(0)
is real and
\detz A
is positive.

Now we move on to the second notion of determinant. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Denote by
\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}
and
\Lambda^{k}A
, respectively, the
k
-th exterior product of
\mathcal{H}
and
A
(see Simon
[
71
]
section 1.5).

Proposition 2.9

(
[
71
]
lemma 3.3)
.

If
A
is trace class on
\mathcal{H}
, then
\Lambda^{k}A
is also trace class on
\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}
with bound on trace norm

\big\lVert\Lambda^{k}A\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\leqslant\frac{1}{k!}\big\lVert A\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}^{k}.

(2.24)

In particular, putting

\detf(\mathbbm{1}+zA)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}z^{k}\ttr_{\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}}(\Lambda^{k}A)

(2.25)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
defines an entire function, and

|\detf(\mathbbm{1}+zA)|\leqslant\exp(|z|\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}).\quad\Box

(2.26)

Definition 2.6
.

Let
A
be a trace class operator on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then the determinant
\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)
given by (
2.25
) for
z=1
is called the
Fredholm determinant
of
\mathbbm{1}+A
.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
71
]
theorem 3.4)
.

The map
A\longmapsto\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)
defines a continuous function on the trace ideal
\mathcal{J}_{1}
with
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
. More precisely,

|\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)-\detf(\mathbbm{1}+B)|\leqslant\left\lVert A-B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\exp(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}+1).\quad\Box

(2.27)

Finally we include the next commutativity result which is relatively more advanced.

Lemma 2.11
.

If
A
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
are such that both
AB
and
BA
are of trace class, then we have

\detf(\mathbbm{1}+AB)=\detf(\mathbbm{1}+BA).

(2.28)

Proof.

The proof is based on the Hadamard product formula for (
2.25
). See Simon
[
71
]
theorem 3.7 and the proof of corollary 3.8 (
AB
and
BA
have the same nonzero eigenvalues), and the references therein.
∎

Factorization Lemma

Lemma 2.12
.

Suppose
A
and
K
are
\Psi
DOs such that both
A
and
A(\mathbbm{1}+K)
satisfy the assumptions of proposition
2.8
and that
\detz(A)
and
\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K))
are defined. Suppose moreover
K
is trace class and there exists smoothing operators
\{K_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
AK_{i}\to AK
in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
(in particular,
AK
is also trace class). Then

\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K))=\detz(A)\detf(\mathbbm{1}+K).

(2.29)

Proof.

We follow Kontsevich and Vishik
[
42
]
proposition 6.4 and take for granted that (
2.29
) holds with
K_{i}
in place of
K
. Our assumptions are tailor-made so that as
i\to\infty
,

\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i}))\longrightarrow\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)).

(2.30)

Indeed, by (
2.20
) we have

\left\lVert(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)-\lambda)^{-1}A(K_{i}-K)(A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})-\lambda)^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\leqslant c|\lambda|^{-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}},

(2.31)

with
c
independent of
i
since a fortiori
AK_{i}\to AK
under
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
. This in particular shows when
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>-1
the integral expression for
A(\mathbbm{1}+K)^{-z}-A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})^{-z}
is a converging Bochner integral valued in the trace ideal
\mathcal{J}_{1}
(note
|\lambda^{-z}|\asymp|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)}
as
\lambda\to-\infty
) and since
\ttr_{L^{2}}
is a continuous functional on
\mathcal{J}_{1}
,

\big|\ttr_{L^{2}}(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)^{-z}-A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})^{-z})\big|\lesssim\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(2.32)

Now by (iii) of proposition
2.8
there is
\frac{1}{2}>\delta>0
so that
\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}
,
\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}
are both holomorphic over
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{\delta}(0)
(for example,
\delta<1/|s|
where
s
is the order of
A
). Thus by (
2.32
) and Cauchy’s estimate

\displaystyle\big|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}^{\prime}(0)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}^{\prime}(0)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta}\sup_{|z|=\delta}\big|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}(z)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\sup_{|z|=\delta}\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-3/2}\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}.

This shows
|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}^{\prime}(0)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}^{\prime}(0)|\to 0
, as we have desired.
∎

The Gluing Formula of Burghelea-Friedlander-Kappeler

Let
(M,g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface with induced metric. Assume proposition
5.7
and decompose
\phi=\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
corresponding to
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}\otimes\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}
. In view of equation (
2.5
), and in parallel

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,\DN\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\varphi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M},

(2.33)

if we assume a “formal Fubini theorem” with respect to the heuristic expressions involving
\mathcal{L}
, namely

\int\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})\phi\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\iint\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{\Sigma},(\Delta+m^{2})\phi_{\Sigma}\rangle_{L^{2}}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D},(\Delta+m^{2})\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\phi_{\Sigma}]\otimes[\mathcal{L}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}],

(2.34)

then we are led to the following relation of the corresponding determinants (volumes) which were first rigorously proved by Burghelea, Friedlander and Kappeler
[
10
]
.

Proposition 2.13

(
[
10
]
theorem B,
[
43
]
theorem 1.1)
.

Let
(M,g)
is a closed Riemannian surface and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface with induced metric. Then

\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{M\setminus\Sigma,D}+m^{2})\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}).\quad\Box

(2.35)

The following version where
\Sigma
dissects
M
such that
M\setminus\Sigma=M^{\circ}_{+}\sqcup M^{\circ}_{-}
is also useful.

Corollary 2.14
.

In the situation as above, we have

\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{M_{+},D}+m^{2})\detz(\Delta_{M_{-},D}+m^{2})\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}).\quad\Box

(2.36)

As we base our analysis on background Gaussian probability measures, the formulae (
2.35
) and (
2.36
) constitute separate ingredients (constants) that needs to be “tuned” for the final gluing result to hold exactly. In fact, it is also reasonable to consider “projective gluing” which allows the freedom for an arbitrary (nonzero) constant to appear in the equation (see remark
6.2
and Segal
[
67
]
page 460).

Quadratic Perturbation = Radon-Nikodym Density

Let
C
be a Gaussian covariance operator of order
-s
on a closed Riemannian manifold
\Sigma
, and denote by
\mu_{C}
the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
. Let
V
be another bounded formally self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
(it could be given by a real symmetric Schwartz kernel). In this section we look at the Gibbs measure

\mathrm{d}\mu(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}(\varphi)}{\int\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}},

(2.37)

which is a Gaussian measure (see proposition
2.16
).

From another perspective we consider Radon-Nikodym densities between mutually absolutely continuous Gaussian measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
. See Bogachev
[
13
]
section 6.4 for a general treatment from this perspective. We shall reproduce a proof following Glimm and Jaffe
[
27
]
section 9.3 for reader’s convenience and adaptation to the current situation.

A principal corollary of the results of this section is the following.

Corollary 2.15
.

Let
\Sigma
be the disjoint union of Riemannian circles, embedded in an ambient Riemannian surface
M
(with or without boundary). Let
\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}
and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
be the two Gaussian measures constructed on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance operators
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
and
(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-1}
(if
M
has boundary, specify the boundary condition to be
B
as in section
4.2
). Then
\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}
and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
are mutually absolutely continuous with Radon-Nikodym density given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=({\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}({\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}}.

(2.38)

The proof is at the end of this section. First we come back to the general case.

Proposition 2.16

(cf.
[
27
]
section 9.3)
.

Let
V:L^{2}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow L^{2}(\Sigma)
be as above and moreover assume

C^{-1}+V\textrm{ is positive,}

(2.39)

and that

\widehat{V}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}C^{\frac{1}{2}}VC^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{ is trace class.}

(2.40)

Then

1.

the random variable
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
can be defined in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
and
\mathbb{E}_{C}[\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}]=\ttr(\widehat{V})
,

2.

Z:=\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]={\textstyle\det_{\mathrm{Fr}}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and

3.

the Gibbs measure (
2.37
) is Gaussian with covariance
(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}
.

Note that since
C^{-1}+V
is positive and
C
is also positive,
\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}=C^{\frac{1}{2}}(C^{-1}+V)C^{\frac{1}{2}}
is positive.

Lemma 2.17
.

There exist an orthonormal basis
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of the Gaussian Hilbert space
W^{-s}(\Sigma)
of
\mu_{C}
equipped with
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, such that

\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2},

(2.41)

for all
\varphi
belonging to the Cameron-Martin space
W^{s}(\Sigma)
, where
\{\lambda_{j}\}
are the eigenvalues of
\widehat{V}
on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and the series converges absolutely in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
. Thus we
define
the random variable
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
with this converging series. Consequently, (i) of proposition
2.16
holds.

Proof.

The key is to seek
\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}
as complete
L^{2}
-orthonormal eigenfunctions of
\widehat{V}
with eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}\}
, which exist since
\widehat{V}
is self-adjoint and trace class on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
. Now
\{f_{j}\}\subset W^{-s}(\Sigma)
and is complete orthonormal with respect to
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. This also means the random variables
\{\varphi(f_{j})\}
are mutually independent. Note for
\varphi\in W^{s}(\Sigma)
,
C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and

\displaystyle\big\langle\varphi,V\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\widehat{V}C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\big|\big\langle C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2}.

Since
\widehat{V}
is trace class,

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{C}\big[|\lambda_{j}|\varphi(f_{j})^{2}\big]=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty,

(2.42)

and we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

We thus defined
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
as a
Wiener quadratic form
and it lies in the second Wiener chaos of
\mu_{C}
, a fortiori in
L^{2}(\mu_{C})
. See Bogachev
[
13
]
pages 257-261 for more information and in particular proposition 5.10.16 for the same result in the context of Malliavin calculus. In fact,

VCf_{j}=C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{V}C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}=\lambda_{j}f_{j},

(2.43)

and hence
V
is trace class on
W^{s}(\Sigma)
with eigenbasis
\{Cf_{j}\}
.

Remark 2.9
.

We have
\lambda_{j}>-1
by the assumption (
2.39
).

To treat (ii) and (iii) of proposition
2.16
we adopt some approximations.

Lemma 2.18
.

Proposition
2.16
(ii) and (iii) is true in the case
V
has finite rank.

Proof.

In this case the series in (
2.41
) is finite with
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}x_{j}^{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}x_{j}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{N}x

\displaystyle=\prod_{j}(1+\lambda_{j})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\det(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

by projecting onto
\mathbb{R}^{N}
via
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi(f_{1}),\dots,\varphi(f_{N}))=:(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.
For the covariance, we orthogonally decompose
W^{-s}(\Sigma)
as

W^{-s}(\Sigma)=\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}\oplus\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}^{\perp},

(2.44)

let
\Pi_{0}
and
\Pi_{1}
be the corresponding orthogonal projections (in order), and for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
write

\varphi(f)=\alpha_{1}\varphi(f_{1})+\cdots+\alpha_{N}\varphi(f_{N})+\varphi(\Pi_{1}f),

(2.45)

then
\varphi(\Pi_{1}f)
is independent of both
\varphi(f_{j})
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant N
, and
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. It is now clear that (
2.37
) is Gaussian because we could now express
\mu(\{\phi(f)\in A\})
for any Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}
as a Gaussian integral over
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
. Apply
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
to (
2.44
) we get the
L^{2}
-orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(\Sigma)=\underbrace{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\})}_{=:L^{2}(\Sigma)_{0}}\oplus\underbrace{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}^{\perp})}_{=:L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}}.

(2.46)

Clearly
\widehat{V}
leaves this decomposition invariant and is zero on
L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}
. Hence
(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}
is block-diagonal,

(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}=\begin{array}[]{ccl}\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}_{1}^{N}&L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}&\\
\diag_{j=1}^{N}\{(\lambda_{j}+1)^{-1}\}&0&\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}_{1}^{N}\\
0&\mathbbm{1}&L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}\\
\end{array}

(2.47)

Thus

\displaystyle Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(f)^{2}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle}]

\displaystyle=Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(\Pi_{0}f)^{2}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2}}]+Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(\Pi_{1}f)^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}+1)^{-1}+\big\langle\Pi_{1}f,C\Pi_{1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{0}f,(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{0}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}+\big\langle\Pi_{1}f,C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}},

where we perform again a Gaussian integral on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
in the second line.
∎

Proof of proposition
2.16
(ii) and (iii).

For general
V
we impose spectral cut-off at
N
,

V_{N}(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\left\langle\varphi,f_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}f_{j}.

(2.48)

Then
C^{\frac{1}{2}}V_{N}C^{\frac{1}{2}}=:\widehat{V}_{N}\to\widehat{V}
under the trace norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\ttr}
(acting on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
). Hence
\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\to\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
by lemma
2.17
and after passing to a subsequence

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\to\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}

(2.49)

in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
and (ii) for
V
follows, since
\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}_{N})\to\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})
by lemma
2.10
.
To prove (iii), we note that
\widehat{V_{N}}\to\widehat{V}
a fortiori under the operator norm, then
C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}_{N})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\to C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}
in norm and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(f)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(f)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V_{N})^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\Big),

for
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, showing that (
2.37
) is Gaussian with the right covariance.
∎

Proof of corollary
2.15
.

Remember now that
\dim\Sigma=1
.
Write for short
\mathbf{D}:=2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
and
\DN:=\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
. Setting
C=\mathbf{D}^{-1}
and
V=\DN-\mathbf{D}
in proposition
2.16
, we are left to prove the determinant identity

\detz(\mathbf{D})\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\mathbf{D}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\DN-\mathbf{D})\mathbf{D}^{-\frac{1}{2}})=\detz(\DN).

(2.50)

Indeed, this is now immediate as

\textrm{LHS}=\detz(\mathbf{D})\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\mathbf{D}^{-1}(\DN-\mathbf{D}))=\textrm{RHS}

(2.51)

by lemma
2.11
and lemma
2.12
. We point out
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{-1}(\DN-\mathbf{D})=V
can be approximated in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
by smoothing operators (by lemma
4.4
(iv),
V=\DN-\mathbf{D}
is
L^{2}
-trace class), so the conditions of lemma
2.12
is satisfied. Indeed, as above,
\{Cf_{j}\}\subset W^{1}(\Sigma)
are
W^{1}
-complete eigenfunctions of
V
. If the corresponding eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}\neq 0
, then the bootstrap argument shows
Cf_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
since
V\in\Psi^{<0}(\Sigma)
. Thus
V_{N}
, where
V_{N}
is as in (
2.48
), approximates
V
in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
and is smoothing. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Though it is probably true, we do not claim
\DN-\mathbf{D}
is elliptic.

Variants of Nelson’s Argument

The goal of this section is to define part
A
of (
2.1
) which culminates in
Nelson’s theorem
. The principal obstacle in achieving this is the fact that powers of a distribution such as
\phi^{2}
,
\phi^{4}
etc., are generally not defined. Even as a random variable under
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, we have (formally)
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(y)]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)
from (
2.6
) for
x\neq y
but this implies
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(x)]=G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,x)=\infty
. This necessitates a procedure of
renormalization
which
subtracts away

\infty
and makes
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(x)]<\infty
.

Example 3.1

(
[
22
]
page 288)
.

A simplest example of such a procedure is defining the product distribution
x^{-1}1_{(0,\infty)}
on
\mathbb{R}
(here
x^{-1}
is the principal value distribution). Note
x^{-1}
and
1_{(0,\infty)}
have a common singularity at
0
, their product is not naturally a distribution over
\mathbb{R}
. However, keeping
\varepsilon>0
away from
0
, for any test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have by integration by parts

\int_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}\varphi(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}=-\int_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(x)\log(x)\mathrm{d}x-\varphi(\varepsilon)\log(\varepsilon).

(3.1)

The point is that, since
\log(x)
is locally integrable, the distribution
\varphi\mapsto-\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(x)\log(x)\mathrm{d}x
is nevertheless well-defined and agrees with
x^{-1}1_{(0,\infty)}
for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
since for small enough
\varepsilon
away from
\supp(\varphi)
the boundary term disappears. Therefore this latter distribution could be taken as a
renormalized version
of
x^{-1}1_{(0,\infty)}
and formally corresponds to
x^{-1}1_{(0,\infty)}-\infty\cdot\delta_{0}
by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
3.1
). (In fact, taking the principal value is in a broad sense another renormalization.)

In our case the natural renormalization strategy is provided by the Gaussian probability theory. Let
\{K_{\varepsilon}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varepsilon>0\}
be a family of
smoothing
operators
5
5

5

that is, each
K_{\varepsilon}
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
.
on
M
, for which
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(in a sense to be specified later), and consider the mollified random field
\phi_{\varepsilon}:=K_{\varepsilon}\phi
. Instead of
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}
, say, we look at

\displaystyle{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}{:}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}-6\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}]\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}+3\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}]^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}-6C_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}+3C_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}.

It happens that for any
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the integral
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
converges as a random variable in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
to a definitive limit, and defines
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
as a random variable in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
. Note that
C_{\varepsilon}(x)\to\infty
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, so we have subtracted “infinities”.

Remark 3.1
.

The crucial point
in our adaptation of Nelson’s argument is to realize the
locality
of the interaction
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
(see section
5.5
). To this end we must allow a sufficiently large class of
regulators

K_{\varepsilon}
(in particular,
local
ones) and show that they define the same interaction (proposition
3.5
). In addition, the
Wick ordering
also needs to be local (see section
3.4
), so that the interaction on a domain with boundary could be defined without reference to the ambient closed manifold where this domain “caps”. See Brunetti, Fredenhagen, Verch
[
7
]
and Guo, Paycha, Zhang
[
31
]
for more information and perspective on locality.

Remark 3.2
.

The method adopted here is restricted to dimension two. In three dimensions, the target measure bearing the heuristic form (
2.1
) becomes mutually singular with respect to
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and hence cannot be expressed as an integrable function multiplied by
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. A recent phenomenal method to treat this case is developed in the framework of stochastic PDEs, called
stochastic quantization
, providing an alternative to older results outlined in
[
27
]
section 23.1. See the introductions in
[
28
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
2
]
,
[
46
]
and
[
5
]
for reviews of the literature and pedagogical discussions.

Regularizations

In this subsection we describe an admissible class of regulators
K_{\varepsilon}
which would eventually produce the same random variable
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
as will be proved in the next subsection. Basically, they are smoothing operators such that
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the
symbol sense
for any
\delta>0
(see definition below). A compact notation is to say
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{0+}(M)
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}
we say that operators
K_{\varepsilon}\to K
in
\Psi^{r}(M)

in the symbol sense
if for any coordinate chart
\kappa:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and cut-off
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
, the full symbol of
\chi K_{\varepsilon}\chi
(considered acting on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\kappa(U))
), converges to that of
\chi K\chi
in the
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}(\kappa(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
topology.

Now we describe the first candidate for
K_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the above assumption (the proof is in appendix
C.2
). This was introduced in Dyatlov and Zworski
[
20
]
and has the advantage of being
local
, realizing the locality of the
P(\phi)
interaction eventually in section
5.5
. Consider
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-1,1))
with
0\leqslant\psi\leqslant 1
and equal to
1
near
0
. For
\varepsilon>0
we define the operator

E_{\varepsilon}u(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{M}E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)u(y)\mathrm{d}V_{g}(y),\quad\textrm{with }E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\frac{1}{F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\psi\left(\frac{d_{g}(x,y)}{\varepsilon}\right).

(3.2)

Here
F_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int\psi(d(x,y)/\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}y
so that
\int E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y=1
, and
d_{g}
denotes the Riemannian distance. One observes that
E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
is smooth for each
\varepsilon>0
so
E_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
. Observe also that
\varepsilon^{d}/C\leqslant F_{\varepsilon}(x)\leqslant C\varepsilon^{d}
for some
C>0
, and this
C
could be made dependent neither on
\varepsilon
nor on
x
as
M
is compact.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
\delta>0
we have
E_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense.

Proof.

See appendix
C.2
.
∎

Note that
K^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=K_{\varepsilon}(y,x)
for real smoothing operators (and their symbols are related in a simple manner),
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in the symbol sense is equivalent to
K_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to\mathbbm{1}
in the symbol sense.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\{E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}>0}
be another family of smoothing operators such that
E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta^{\prime}}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
. Then the net
E_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}(E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}-E_{\varepsilon})
,
(\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
(we say
(\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon)\prec(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
iff
\varepsilon^{\prime}>\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1}
and
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{1}
), converges to zero in
\Psi^{-2+\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
.

Proof.

Note that following essentially the same arguments as above the
\Psi^{\delta/2}(M)
seminorms of
E_{\varepsilon}
can be bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
. This said, the result follows essentially from the continuity of the twisted product (composition product) of symbols as a map
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}\times\mathcal{S}^{r^{\prime}}_{1,0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{r+r^{\prime}}_{1,0}
with respect to the symbol topologies (see Folland
[
23
]
page 105 theorem 2.47).
∎

We shall consider another set of seminorms on
\Psi^{r}(M)
in the case
-d<r<0
which suits better our purposes. They are defined as follows. Let
\mathcal{M}\subset C^{\infty}(M\times M,T(M\times M))
denote the
C^{\infty}(M\times M)
-module of smooth vector fields tangent to the diagonal in
M\times M
. We fix a finite coordinate cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in 1}^{N}
of
M
, with charts
\kappa_{i}:U_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\}
subordinate to this cover.

Definition 3.2
.

For any
K\in C^{\infty}(M\times M\setminus\mathrm{diag})
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
and
L_{1}
, …,
L_{p}\in\mathcal{M}
, we define the seminorms

p_{i,L_{1},\dots,L_{p}}(K)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sup_{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}}\big|(\kappa_{i}\times\kappa_{i})_{*}\left((\chi_{i}\otimes\chi_{i})L_{1}\cdots L_{p}K\right)(x,y)\big|\cdot d_{g}(x,y)^{d+r},

(3.3)

while on
U_{i}\times U_{j}
,
i\neq j
, which does not touch the diagonal, we use the
C^{\infty}(U_{i}\times U_{j})
seminorms. By the
kernel topology
on
\Psi^{r}(M)
,
-d<r<0
, we mean the topology induced by these seminorms on the Schwartz kernels
K_{A}
of
A\in\Psi^{r}(M)
. Here
d_{g}
is the distance function.

Proposition 3.3
.

In the case
-d<r<0
, the above kernel topology is equivalent to the topology induced by symbols
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}(T^{*}M)
on
\Psi^{r}(M)
. In particular, if
A_{\varepsilon}\to A
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in
\Psi^{r}(M)
in the symbol sense then
A_{\varepsilon}\to A
also in the above kernel topology.

Proof.

Essentially in Taylor
[
78
]
page 6, proposition 2.2, page 7, proposition 2.4 and page 10, proposition 2.7. See also Bailleul, Dang, Ferdinand and Tô
[
6
]
proposition 6.9 for a more detailed treatment.
∎

Finally, we observe that the heat operator
K_{\varepsilon}=\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}
is also a valid candidate:

Lemma 3.4

(
[
16
]
lemma 4.15)
.

We have
\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
.
\Box

Some properties of the heat operator is summed up in appendix
C.2
.

Integrability of Interaction and Regularization Independence

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
(K_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be any family of real smoothing operators such that
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
. Define
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)
and
{:}P(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)){:}
as above and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
be a test function. Put

S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}(\phi):=\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}.

(3.4)

This is a random variable on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
equipped with
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. Then
\{S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\}
converges in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and the limit is independent of the specific smoothing
(K_{\varepsilon})
chosen, provided they have the convergence property described above.

More precisely, for any other smoothing family
(\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})
satisfying the same condition and defining the random variable
\widetilde{S}_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime},\chi}
, we have a quantitative bound of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big|S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}-\widetilde{S}_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime},\chi}\big|^{2}\big]\leqslant\mathfrak{O}(|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|)C_{n}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{O}(|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|)
is a function going to zero as
|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|\to 0
, depending only on
M
.

A particularity of the dimension two is seen in the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\dim M=2
and
g
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
M
then

\iint_{M\times M}|\log(d_{g}(x,y))|^{p}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{p}<\infty

(3.5)

for any
1\leqslant p<\infty
and

\iint_{M\times M}d_{g}(x,y)^{-\delta}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{\delta}<\infty

(3.6)

for any
0<\delta<1
,
d_{g}
denoting the distance function.
\Box

Consequently, since in two dimensions
G(x,y)=\mathcal{O}(\log(d(x,y)))
as
y\to x
(see lemma
3.12
), a simple argument with partition of unity shows

\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)|G(x,y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{p}\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\vol(\text{supp}(\chi))^{2}<\infty

(3.7)

for some
C_{p}>0
, for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
. When
p=2
this is the familiar fact that in two dimensions the Green operator
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
is Hilbert-Schmidt, which may also be shown using Weyl’s law.

We will denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},K_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}K_{\varepsilon}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y),

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},K_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},\tilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y).

(3.10)

Note that
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
has a
different
regulator on the second variable! For fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we have by lemma
B.9
,

{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}=G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{n}h_{2n}\big(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\big/G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\geqslant-b_{1}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{n},

(3.11)

for some constant
b_{1}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
, since the even-degree Hermite polynomial
h_{2n}
is bounded below.

Proof of proposition
3.5
.

We shall prove the proposition for the case
P(\theta)=\theta^{2n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the general case is similar. For fixed
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we compute

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{M,\varepsilon}-S_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left|\int_{M}\chi{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}-\int_{M}\chi{:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}{:}\chi(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle=\iint_{M\times M}\Big(\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]-2\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]
