\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]\Big)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

(Tonelli)

\displaystyle=(2n)!\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left(G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n}-2G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}+G_{\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}\right)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

(lemma
B.9
)

We will control the integral
\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left(G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n}-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}\right)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}
for
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\chi\geqslant 0
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle|\textrm{this integral}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)\right|\cdot\big|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n-2}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)+\dots+G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n-1}\big|\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2n\|\chi\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left(C_{8n-4}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\times\left(\iint_{M\times M}\left|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Remember that
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)
is the kernel of
K^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)
is the kernel of
K^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}(\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}-K_{\varepsilon})
. By lemma
3.2
, definition
3.2
and proposition
3.3
,

|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)|\leqslant C_{M,\delta}d_{g}(x,y)^{-\delta},

(3.12)

uniformly in
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime})
and by lemma
3.2
,

|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)|\leqslant\mathfrak{O}_{M,\delta}(|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|)d_{g}(x,y)^{-\delta}

(3.13)

for any
\delta>0
. If we restrict moreover to
\delta<1
, then we prove our result, thanks to lemma
3.6
.
∎

Integrability of the Exponential of Interaction

In this subsection we will adopt the heat regulator
K_{\varepsilon}=\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon}:=\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\deg P=2n
. Then
S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and hence the resulting limit
S_{M,\chi}
, is in the
(2n)
-th Wiener chaos of the GFF, that is,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{2n}(\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}=W^{-1}(M)
is the Gaussian Hilbert space of the GFF.

Proof.

We shall show it for
P(\theta)=\theta^{4}
and the general case is similar. Here we use the spectral representation of remark
2.2
(using the notations thereof) and take
K_{\varepsilon}:=\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}
. Thus we can write

\displaystyle\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\langle\varphi_{j},K_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\xi_{j}\bigg)^{4}=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\lambda_{j}+m^{2})}\varphi_{j}(x)\xi_{j}\bigg)^{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,\ell,p}\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{p}+4m^{2})}\varphi_{j}(x)\varphi_{k}(x)\varphi_{\ell}(x)\varphi_{p}(x)\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\xi_{\ell}\xi_{p},

the series converging absolutely in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
. Now each individual term is clearly in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(\mathcal{H})
. Since

\int|\varphi_{j}(x)\varphi_{k}(x)\varphi_{\ell}(x)\varphi_{p}(x)|\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)\lesssim\vol(M)\cdot[\textrm{polynomial in }\lambda_{j},\lambda_{k},\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{p}\textrm{ with fixed degree}]

(3.14)

as one has
\sup_{M}|\varphi_{j}|\lesssim(1+\lambda_{j})^{2}
in two dimensions which follows essentially from the Sobolev embedding (see Sogge
[
75
]
page 43 equation (3.1.12)), thus

\int\chi(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)=\sum_{j,k,\ell,p}\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{p}+4m^{2})}C_{\chi,j,k,\ell,p}\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\xi_{\ell}\xi_{p}

(3.15)

with the series converging absolutely in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
and the result is in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(\mathcal{H})
.
∎

Proposition 3.8

(hypercontractivity,
[
40
]
theorem 5.10,
[
72
]
theorem I.22)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a Gaussian Hilbert space on some probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
, and let
n\geqslant 1
,
2\leqslant p<\infty
. Then

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{p}]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leqslant(p-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.16)

for all
X\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(\mathcal{H})
.
\Box

Combining lemma
3.7
and proposition
3.8
, we have

Corollary 3.9
.

The convergence of
\{S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\}
, as well as the limit
S_{M,\chi}
, is in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}})
for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
. Moreover, if
\chi\rightarrow 0
in
L^{4}(M)
then

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\chi\rightarrow 0}S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}=0

as random variable in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
.
Moreover
\mathbb{E}[\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}}]
remains uniformly bounded along the limit. In particular
S_{M,\chi}
is defined for
\chi\in L^{4}(M)
.
\Box

We single out a calculus computation which will be used in the sequel:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
a
,
b
be positive real numbers. Then the real function
\alpha(x):=x^{(bx)}a^{x}
,
x>0
, attains its minimum value
\textrm{e}^{-be^{-1}a^{-1/b}}
at
x=e^{-1}a^{-1/b}
.
\Box

Theorem

(Nelson)
.

We have for
\chi\in L^{4}(M)
,

\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\chi}}\in L^{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}})

(3.17)

and hence

Z_{M}={\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}(\Delta_{g}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}{:}P(\phi){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}(\phi)<\infty.

(3.18)

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, by (
3.7
) and (
3.11
),

S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\geqslant-b\vol(\text{supp}(\chi))\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sup_{x}(|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)|^{n}).

(3.19)

From formula (
C.16
), for
\varepsilon
small,

G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)=\int_{2\varepsilon}^{\infty}p_{t}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t=\bigg(\underbrace{\int_{2\varepsilon}^{1}}_{A}+\underbrace{\int_{1}^{\infty}}_{B}\bigg)p_{t}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t.

(3.20)

Now by (iii) of lemma
C.5
part
A
is
\mathcal{O}(\log(2\varepsilon))
; since our field is
massive
(
m>0
), by (iv) of lemma
C.5
part
B
is
bounded
. Therefore one has overall
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)=\mathcal{O}(\log(2\varepsilon))
.
As a result
S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\geqslant-b_{2}|\log(2\varepsilon)|^{n}
for
\varepsilon
small.

Now we compute that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\chi}}\geqslant\textrm{e}^{b_{2}|\log(2\varepsilon)|^{n}+1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(S_{M,\chi}\leqslant-b_{2}|\log(2\varepsilon)|^{n}-1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(|S_{M,\chi}-S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}|\geqslant 1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert S_{M,\chi}-S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}})}^{p}

(Chebyshev)

\displaystyle\leqslant(p-1)^{\frac{np}{2}}C_{1}^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{p}{2}}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}^{p}

(proposition
3.8
and
3.5
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}^{p}p^{\frac{n}{2}p}(C_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})^{p},

for all
2\leqslant p<\infty
. The last line as a function of
p
has the form dealt with in lemma
3.10
and attains a minimum of
\exp\big(-C_{2}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}})^{-1/n}\big)
, with some absorbed constant
C_{2}>0
which does not depend on
\chi
. Thus we obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\chi}}\geqslant\textrm{e}^{b_{2}|\log(2\varepsilon)|^{n}+1}\big)\lesssim\exp\big(-C_{2}\big(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}\big)^{-\frac{1}{n}}\big).

(3.21)

Now
we may conclude with the formula

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[e^{-S_{M,\chi}}\big]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\chi}}\geqslant t\right)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\left(\textrm{e}^{-S_{M,\chi}}\geqslant t\right)\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|\mathrm{d}\varepsilon

where the last integral involves a change of variable
t:=\textrm{e}^{b_{2}|\log(2\varepsilon)|^{n}+1}
. This gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[e^{-S_{M,\chi}}\big]\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{1}\exp\big(-C_{2}\big(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}\big)^{-\frac{1}{n}}\big)C_{3}\varepsilon^{-1}n|\log(\varepsilon)|^{n-1}e^{c|\log(\varepsilon)|^{n}-1}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon

which is finite since integrable near
\varepsilon=0
.
Moreover, we see that the bound is uniform when
\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}\leqslant C_{0}
for some given
C_{0}>0
.
∎

Change of Wick Ordering

In order for the proof of proposition
3.5
to work as it is written one has to insist on the Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}
provided by
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, since we desire convergence in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
and with a different Wick ordering the Feynman rules (lemma
B.9
) are not exact. Nevertheless, in order to define the interaction over a domain
\Omega
independently of its embedding in an ambient manifold
M
, one must employ a Wick ordering independent of
M
, or in order words, one that is
local
.

Let
d
denote the Riemannian distance function of
M
. This function is local in the sense that
d(x,y)
(as
y\to x
) depends only on the restriction of the Riemannian metric on any geodesic convex neighborhood containing
x
and
y
. The local Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}_{0}
is provided by the
log-measure

\mu_{\log}^{M}
which is the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
with covariance

\mathbb{E}_{\log}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\int_{M}f(x)\Big(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(m\cdot d(x,y))\Big)h(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(y),

(3.22)

for
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
, thanks to lemma
3.6
. Here
m
is the mass used for
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
. We denote
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(md(x,y)):=C_{0}(x,y)
and the corresponding operator by
C_{0}
. We emphasize here that
\mu_{\log}^{M}
is used only as a tool to produce a linear change of random variables with deterministic coefficients, no random variables will be actually defined on
\mu_{\log}^{M}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

If
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are two covariance operators on
M
, then

{:}\phi(f)^{n}{:}_{C_{1}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{n!}{(n-2j)!j!2^{j}}\left\langle f,(C_{2}-C_{1})f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}^{j}{:}\phi(f)^{n-2j}{:}_{C_{2}},

(3.23)

for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

Proof.

Follows readily from Wick’s theorem.
∎

The reason why the new Wick ordering works is the following. Let
G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)
denote the integral kernel of
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

For each
x\in M
, the limit

\lim_{y\to x}\left(G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)-C_{0}(x,y)\right)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\delta G(x)

(3.24)

exists, and that
\delta G\in L^{p}(M)
for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
.

Remarks for proof.

The function
\delta G
is called in our context the (point-splitting)
tadpole function
(see Kandel, Mnev and Wernli
[
37
]
section 5.4, in particular lemma 5.20 for a precise expression), which can be seen as a
renormalized
diagonal value of the Green function
G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,x)
. The asymptotic of the Green function along the diagonal is a classical subject and we have in fact
G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)-C_{0}(x,y)\in C^{1}(M\times M)
. The function
\delta G
is also important in the context of conformal geometry where it is called the
mass function
, if more precisely we do not include the constant
-\log m/2\pi
in
C_{0}
but rather in
\delta G
. See Hermann and Humbert
[
34
]
, Ludewig
[
45
]
or Schoen and Yau
[
65
]
for more information.
∎

It follows that

\left\langle E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x},\left((\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}-C_{0}\right)E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}\longrightarrow\delta G(x)

(3.25)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
and through the limiting process of proposition
3.5
we find

\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi(x)^{2n}{:}_{0}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{(2n)!}{(2n-2j)!j!2^{j}}\int\chi(x)\delta G(x)^{j}{:}\phi(x)^{2n-2j}{:}_{\mathrm{GFF}}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x),

(3.26)

which exist as a random variable in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
for all
2\leqslant p<\infty
since each term on the RHS are such by proposition
3.5
and corollary
3.9
.

The Trace Operator and the Poisson Integral Operator

In this section we obtain a series of rather elementary relations between various geometric-analytic operators on
M
and on
\Omega
. The moral is that, the so-called “sharp-time localization” map
j_{\Sigma}
(see lemma
4.5
), induced probability laws of Gaussian fields under the trace
\tau_{\Sigma}
(see section
4.4
), and finally the Green-Stokes formula, are largely different aspects of the same thing.

Summary of Essential Properties

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold, and
\Sigma\subset M
a smooth embedded hypersurface (codimension one submanifold). Then we call the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\tau_{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(M)&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\Sigma),\\
f&\longmapsto&f|_{\Sigma},\end{array}\right.

(4.1)

the
trace map
(or
restriction map
) from
M
onto
\Sigma
.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
77
]
page 334,
[
21
]
page 57, example 13.3)
.

Let
(M,g)
and
\Sigma
be as above. Then

1.

the map
\tau_{\Sigma}
extends uniquely to a continuous operator
\tau_{\Sigma}:W^{s}(M)\longrightarrow W^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
for each
s>\frac{1}{2}
;

2.

the map
\tau_{\Sigma}:W^{s}(M)\longrightarrow W^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
,
s>\frac{1}{2}
, is surjective.

Remark for proof.

For (ii), the case of
M=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is treated in
[
21
]
as indicated above. One could then supply a partition of unity argument to extend to the current case, remembering that
\Sigma
is smoothly embedded.
∎

Now let
(\Omega,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial\Omega
. It is well-known that for
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
, the (Helmholtz) boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta_{\Omega}+m^{2})u=0,&\textrm{in }\Omega,\\
u|_{\partial\Omega}=f,&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(4.2)

admits a unique solution
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
which is extendably smooth upto
\partial\Omega
.

Remark 4.1
.

In this paper, a function
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is said to satisfy the
Dirichlet condition
(respectively
Neumann
) if
u|_{\partial\Omega}=0
(respectively
(\partial_{\nu}u)|_{\partial\Omega}=0
,
\nu
the outward unit normal). The
f
appearing in (
4.2
) is called a
Dirichlet datum
.

Definition 4.2
.

We call the operator

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}:C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}),\\
f&\longmapsto&u,\end{array}\right.

(4.3)

where
u
is the unique solution of (
4.2
), the
Poisson integral operator
(or
Harmonic extension operator
) from
\partial\Omega
to
\Omega
,
with mass

m>0
.

Lemma 4.2

(
[
77
]
page 361, proposition 1.8)
.

Let
(\Omega,g)
be as above. Then
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
extends uniquely to a continuous operator

\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}:W^{s}(\partial\Omega)\longrightarrow W^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)

(4.4)

for each
s\geqslant-\frac{1}{2}
.
\Box

We shall need a variant of the Poisson integral operator that works for embedded hypersurfaces in closed manifolds. Let
(M,g)
and
\Sigma
be as in definition
4.1
. Pick
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. This time we look at the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})u=0,&\textrm{in }M\setminus\Sigma,\\
u|_{\Sigma}=f,&\textrm{on }\Sigma.\end{array}\right.

(4.5)

Indeed, one should view
M\setminus\Sigma
as a manifold with two boundaries
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
, and as a result one obtains a unique solution
u
which is smooth on
M\setminus\Sigma
and one-sidedly smooth upto
\Sigma
respectively on its two sides.

Definition 4.3
.

We call the operator

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(\Sigma)&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\overline{M\setminus\Sigma}),\\
f&\longmapsto&u,\end{array}\right.

(4.6)

where
u
is the unique solution of (
4.5
), the
Poisson integral operator
from
\Sigma
to
M
,
with mass

m>0
.

Definition 4.4
.

More generally if
\Omega
has boundaries and
\Sigma
is
either
one component of
\partial\Omega

or
embedded in the
interior
of
\Omega
, then we denote by
\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}f
the solution with its restriction equal to
f
on
\Sigma
and boundary condition “
B
” (Dirichlet or Neumann) on all components of
\partial\Omega

except

\Sigma
. Such notations raise no ambiguity when the situation is understood from context.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(M,g)
and
\Sigma
be as above. Then
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}
extends uniquely to a continuous operator

\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}:W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow W^{1}(M).

(4.7)

Proof.

Let
f\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
and
u:=\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f
. We know that
u\in W^{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M\setminus\Sigma)
, this means
u\in L^{2}(M\setminus\Sigma)=L^{2}(M)
, and
\nabla u\in L^{2}(T(M\setminus\Sigma))
, as a distribution over
M\setminus\Sigma
. The problem is to compute
\nabla u
as a distribution over
M
. For this, one picks a testing vector field
X\in C^{\infty}(M,TM)
and applies the Green-Stokes formula to get

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla u,X\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,TM)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}-\left\langle u,\ddiv X\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\int_{M\setminus\Sigma}\left\langle\nabla u,X\right\rangle_{g}\mathrm{d}V_{M}-\int_{\Sigma}f\left\langle X,\nu\right\rangle_{g}\mathrm{d}V_{\Sigma}-\int_{\Sigma}f\left\langle X,-\nu\right\rangle_{g}\mathrm{d}V_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{M\setminus\Sigma}\left\langle\nabla u,X\right\rangle_{g}\mathrm{d}V_{M}.

Here
\nu
is any one of the two possible unit normal vector fields along
\Sigma
. This shows that, nevertheless,

\nabla^{M}u=\nabla^{M\setminus\Sigma}u,

(4.8)

and hence
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{W^{1}(M)}=\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(T(M\setminus\Sigma))}+\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}\approx\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{W^{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)}
.
∎

The Dirichlet-to-Neumann Operator and its Jumpy Version

Definition 4.5
.

Let
(\Omega,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial\Omega\neq\varnothing
, and
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}:C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)\to C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
the Poisson operator defined previously. Put

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}:C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega),\\
f&\longmapsto&\partial_{\nu}(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}f),\end{array}\right.

(4.9)

where
\nu=
outward unit normal along
\partial\Omega
, called the
Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
on
\partial\Omega
with respect to
\Omega
.

Remark 4.2
.

As its name suggests,
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
computes the associated Neumann data of the solution to the Helmholtz equation with given Dirichlet data. By the well-posedness and regularity of the Neumann problem, one could also do it the other way round: compute the Dirichlet data out of a Neumann data. In this way one sees that
(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}
can be defined and maps
C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold,
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded hypersurface, and
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)\to C^{\infty}(M\setminus\Sigma)
the hypersurface Poisson operator. Put

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega),\\
f&\longmapsto&\partial_{\nu}(\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f)|_{\Sigma_{-}}+\partial_{-\nu}(\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f)|_{\Sigma_{+}},\end{array}\right.

(4.10)

where
\nu
is any one of the two unit normal vector fields along
\Sigma
, extended over a cylindrical neighborhood of
\Sigma
. Here
\Sigma_{-}
and
\Sigma_{+}
means that we are taking one-sided derivatives, respectively, from the backward-time and forward-time directions with regard to the flow of
\nu
. We call
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
the
jumpy Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
on
\Sigma
with respect to
M
.

Definition 4.7
.

Similarly if
\Omega
has boundaries and
\Sigma
is
either
one component of
\partial\Omega

or
embedded in the
interior
of
\Omega
, then we denote by
\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}
the corresponding operator with
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}
replaced by
\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}
in the definition (see definition
4.4
).

Remark 4.3
.

If we see
M\setminus\Sigma
as a manifold with boundary
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
, then
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}f
is also the sum over
\Sigma
of the two
outward
unit normal derivatives of
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f
along
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
. Intuitively,
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}f
describes the “jump” of
\nabla\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f
across
\Sigma
, whence its name.

We summarize in the following lemma the essential properties of
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
and
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
. Parallel results also hold for
\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma,B}
(
\Sigma
being either one component of boundary or embedded in interior) but we shall not discuss them in order to simplify the presentation. The same applies to everything below this section.

Lemma 4.4
.

Under their respective settings,
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
and
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
are such that

1.

their quadratic forms are given respectively by the Dirichlet energies of their harmonic extensions:

\displaystyle\big\langle f,\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\big(|\nabla\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}f|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}f)^{2}\big)\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\big\langle f,\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\big(|\nabla\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}(\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}f)^{2}\big)\mathrm{d}V_{M},

(4.12)

for
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
;

2.

they are formally self-adjoint, strictly positive, and
L^{2}
-invertible;

3.

they are elliptic
\Psi
DOs of order
1
, with principal symbols being
|\xi|_{g}
and
2|\xi|_{g}
respectively;

4.

they afford a finer comparison with
\mathbf{D}_{\partial\Omega}=(\Delta_{\partial\Omega}+m^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
or
2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}=2(\Delta_{\Sigma}+m^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
: the operators

\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}-\mathbf{D}_{\partial\Omega},\quad\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma},\quad\mathbf{D}_{\partial\Omega}^{-1}\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}-\mathbbm{1},\quad\textrm{and}\quad(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-1}\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{1},

(4.13)

are
\Psi
DOs of orders at most
-2
,
-2
,
-3
, and
-3
respectively. A fortiori, they are all of trace class when
\dim\Omega=\dim M=2
and
\dim\Sigma=\dim\partial\Omega=1
.

Proof.

See Taylor
[
79
]
and the references therein. For (iv) see Kandel, Mnev and Wernli
[
37
]
proposition A.3.
∎

Two Consequences of the Green-Stokes Formula

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded hypersurface. Formula (
4.12
) in a slightly more general form allows one to obtain an expression for the “distributional adjoint” of the trace map onto
\Sigma
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\tau_{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,
\phi\mapsto\phi|_{\Sigma}
be the trace map. One has, for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

\left\langle\tau_{\Sigma}\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}=\left\langle\phi,j_{\Sigma}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

(4.14)

where
j_{\Sigma}=(\Delta+m^{2})\PI_{M}(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
. Moreover, this equality can be extended to
\phi\in W^{1}(M)
and
f\in W^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

First suppose
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, then applying the Green-Stokes formula to
M\setminus\Sigma
with boundary
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\tau\phi,\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\int_{M\setminus\Sigma}(\langle\nabla\phi,\nabla\PI_{M}h\rangle+m^{2}\phi(\PI_{M}h))\mathrm{d}V_{M}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\langle\nabla\phi,\nabla\PI_{M}h\rangle+m^{2}\phi(\PI_{M}h))\mathrm{d}V_{M}

(lemma
4.3
)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\phi,(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}.

(#)

We remark that step (#) is the
definition
of the action of
(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})
on the distribution
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}h
. By lemma
4.1
, lemma
4.3
, and (iii) of lemma
4.4
, then, this equality can be extended to
\phi\in W^{1}(M)
and
h\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
. Finally, replacing
h
by
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}f
, with
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
being a
\Psi
DO of order
-1
, yields the desired relation (
4.14
) as well as its domain.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

One is advised to compare lemma
4.5
with the fact in one dimensions that the distributional derivative of the Heaviside function
H_{a}=a\cdot 1_{(0,\infty)}
(
a\in\mathbb{R}
) is the delta function multiplied by the jump of
H_{a}
across
0
, that is,

\left\langle H_{a}^{\prime},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=[H_{a}(0+)-H_{a}(0-)]\cdot\varphi(0)

(4.15)

for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. In our case the role of the Heaviside function is played by the vector field
\nabla\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}f
. Indeed, following remark
4.3
, the “jump” of
\nabla\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}f
across
\Sigma
is exactly
f
, as the directions tangential to
\Sigma
does not contribute to the jump with
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}f
being smooth. This comparison in mind, it is also customary to write
j_{\Sigma}f
as
f\otimes\delta_{\Sigma}
, as for example, in Carron
[
14
]
.

Corollary 4.6
.

For
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, we have

\left\langle f,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=\left\langle j_{\Sigma}f,(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}j_{\Sigma}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}.

(4.16)

In other words,
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}=\tau(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}j_{\Sigma}\penalty\ (=\tau(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\tau^{*})
.

Proof.

This is immediate by noting that
\tau_{\Sigma}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}
is the identity on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Indeed, noting that the Schwartz kernel
K_{\tau}
of
\tau_{\Sigma}
is the delta distribution on the diagonal
\{(x,x)\}\subset\Sigma\times M
, and that
j_{\Sigma}
is the distributional adjoint of
\tau_{\Sigma}
, corollary
4.6
allows one to deduce immediately the Schwartz kernel of
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
, denoted
G_{\DN}^{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle f,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Sigma\times M}\iint_{\Sigma\times M}f(x)K_{\tau}(x,z)G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(z,w)K_{\tau}(y,w)h(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Sigma\times\Sigma}f(x)G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)h(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

that is,
G_{\DN}^{\Sigma}=G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}|_{\Sigma\times\Sigma}
, where
G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}
is the Helmholtz Green function on
M
, which is a well-known result. Of course, assuming this result, one could also work backwards to give lemma
4.5
another proof, using the Poisson integral formula (lemma
4.7
below) for
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}
.

Now we move to the second consequence of the Green-Stokes formula. Let
(\Omega,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial\Omega
. Recall that
(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}
denotes the Helmholtz Green operator with
Dirichlet
conditions on
\partial\Omega
.

Lemma 4.7

(
[
78
]
page 46)
.

We have, for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
,

\big\langle\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi,f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big\langle\varphi,-\partial_{\nu}(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}f|_{\partial\Omega}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

(4.17)

where
\nu
, again, denotes the outward unit normal vector field along
\partial\Omega
.
\Box

Corollary 4.8
.

For
(M,g)
a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded hypersurface, for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\setminus\Sigma)
,

\left\langle\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}\varphi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=\left\langle\varphi,-(\partial_{\nu}u|_{\Sigma_{-}}+\partial_{-\nu}u|_{\Sigma_{+}})\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

(4.18)

where
u=(\Delta_{M\setminus\Sigma,D}+m^{2})^{-1}f
, and the notations
\Sigma_{-}
and
\Sigma_{+}
have the same meanings as in definition
4.6
.

Proof.

See
M\setminus\Sigma
as a manifold with two boundaries
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
, and we note

\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}\varphi=\PI_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi},

(4.19)

as well as

\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi},-\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\partial_{\nu}u|_{\Sigma_-}}{\partial_{-\nu}u|_{\Sigma_+}}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma)}=\big\langle\varphi,-(\partial_{\nu}u|_{\Sigma_{-}}+\partial_{-\nu}u|_{\Sigma_{+}})\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

(4.20)

while applying lemma
4.7
.
∎

Induced Laws

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded hypersurface. Lemma
4.5
then says that, for each
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, the random variables

\tau_{\Sigma}\phi(f)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\phi(j_{\Sigma}f),

(4.21)

while
\phi\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, are (surely) equal on the Cameron-Martin space
W^{1}(M)
. By proposition
B.6
then, they are almost surely equal over
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
. Subsequently from corollary
4.6
we deduce

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\tau_{\Sigma}\phi(f)\tau_{\Sigma}\phi(h)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(j_{\Sigma}f)\phi(j_{\Sigma}h)]=\left\langle f,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

(4.22)

Taking into account lemma
2.4
and (iii) of lemma
4.4
, we have proved the following.

Proposition 4.9
.

If
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
follows the law of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, then the random field
\tau_{\Sigma}\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
can equivalently be realized as the (centered) Gaussian field
\widetilde{\varphi}
on
\Sigma
with covariance

\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{\varphi}(f)\widetilde{\varphi}(h)\big]=\left\langle f,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

(4.23)

for
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. In other words, the measure image
\widehat{\tau_{\Sigma}}_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
under any measurable linear extension
\widehat{\tau_{\Sigma}}
of
\tau_{\Sigma}:W^{1}(M)\longrightarrow W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
coincides with the measure
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}
on any
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
for the field
\widetilde{\varphi}
satisfying (
4.23
).
\Box

Next we study induced random fields in the other direction, by the Poisson integral operator. Namely, for
\Omega
,
\partial\Omega
as in lemma
4.7
, given a Gaussian random field
\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
, what is the law of the field
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi
? From another perspective one solves the Helmholtz (Laplace) equation with random boundary conditions. We write in shorthand

(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}-\partial_{\nu}(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}(-)|_{\partial\Omega}.

(4.24)

Thus lemma
4.7
says

\big\langle\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi,f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big\langle\varphi,(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

(4.25)

for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
,
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. Suppose
\varphi
has covariance operator
C
of order
-s
(
s>0
). Note
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
is always well-defined on the Cameron-Martin space
W^{s}(\partial\Omega)
. By the same token as above, for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
,

(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi)(f)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\varphi((\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}f)

(4.26)

are surely equal on
W^{s}(\partial\Omega)
. Moreover,

\mathbb{E}_{C}^{\partial\Omega}\big[(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi)(f)(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi)(h)\big]=\big\langle f,\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}C(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

(4.27)

We deduce

Proposition 4.10
.

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega)
is a (centered) Gaussian random field with covariance operator
C
, then the random field
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
can equivalently be realized as the (centered) Gaussian field
\widetilde{\phi}
on
\Omega
with covariance

\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{\phi}(f)\widetilde{\phi}(h)\big]=\big\langle f,\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}C(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

(4.28)

for
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
. In other words, the measure image
\widehat{\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}}_{*}(\mu_{C}^{\partial\Omega})
of
\mu_{C}^{\partial\Omega}
under any measurable linear extension
\widehat{\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}}
of
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}:W^{s}(\partial\Omega)\longrightarrow W^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)
coincides with the measure for the field
\widetilde{\phi}
satisfying (
4.28
).
\Box

Remark 4.6
.

What is strictly needed for showing Segal axioms is not the full proposition
4.10
but rather this innocent observation: by (
4.19
), if the random field
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
follows a fixed probability law, then the induced random fields
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}\varphi
and
\PI_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}]
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M\setminus\Sigma)
follows the same law.

First Comment on Reflection Positivity

As the names would suggest, the positivity of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map (itself the consequence of the positivity of the Dirichlet energy) gives an interesting inequality comparing the resolvants of Laplacians with Dirichlet and Neumann conditions (corollary
4.12
), via the Poisson integral formula (lemma
4.7
). Let
\Omega
,
\partial\Omega
be as in lemma
4.7
. We adopt the shorthand notations

\quad C_{D}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1},\quad C_{N}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\Delta_{\Omega,N}+m^{2})^{-1},

(4.29)

where, similar to
C_{D}
,
C_{N}f
for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
solves the Neumann boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})(C_{N}f)=f,&\textrm{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{\nu}(C_{N}f)|_{\partial\Omega}=0,&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(4.30)

There is the following simple, elementary relation:

Lemma 4.11
.

In the situation as above, we have the operator equality on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
,

\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}=C_{N}-C_{D}.

(4.31)

Proof.

Pick
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, let
u:=C_{D}f
and put
w:=\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}(-\partial_{\nu}u|_{\partial\Omega})
. Then, by the definition of
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
,
w
solves the following boundary value problem:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})w=0,&\textrm{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{\nu}w|_{\partial\Omega}=-\partial_{\nu}u|_{\partial\Omega},&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(4.32)

However,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})u=f,&\textrm{in }\Omega,\\
u|_{\partial\Omega}=0,&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.\quad\textrm{implying}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})(u+w)=f,&\textrm{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{\nu}(u+w)|_{\partial\Omega}=0,&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(4.33)

namely
u+w=C_{N}f
, that is,
w=C_{N}f-C_{D}f
. We obtain the result.
∎

Now the positivity of
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
(lemma
4.4
) implies
C_{N}\geqslant C_{D}
, namely
\left\langle f,(C_{N}-C_{D})f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\geqslant 0
for all
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. One step further,

Corollary 4.12
.

We have
C_{N}\geqslant C_{D}
as operators on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
\Box

It is emphasized in Jaffe and Ritter
[
38
]
section 3 that
C_{N}\geqslant C_{D}
is the crucial relation that leads to the so-called
reflection positivity
(RP) of the GFF (see definition
5.2
). At this point let us explain its geometric incarnation. Let now
\partial\Omega\subset\Omega
be totally geodesic,
\Omega^{*}
a copy of
\Omega
(reversing the coorientation of
\partial\Omega
),
|\Omega|^{2}:=\Omega^{*}\cup_{\partial\Omega}\Omega
, the
isometric double
which is a closed Riemannian manifold, and
\Theta:|\Omega|^{2}\longrightarrow|\Omega|^{2}
an isometric involution fixing
\partial\Omega
, such that
\Theta(\Omega)=\Omega^{*}
and
\Theta(\Omega^{*})=\Omega
.

Remark 4.7
.

Such
\Omega
is named in Gibbons
[
24
]
(in the 4-dimensional case) as a
real tunnelling geometry
(see
[
24
]
section 4). The isometric double
|\Omega|^{2}
and the involution
\Theta
exist, the latter being a
reflection
,
dissecting

|\Omega|^{2}
into
\Omega
and
\Omega^{*}
, its fixed point set being
\partial\Omega
(see also Ritter
[
59
]
section 2.1.1).

The action of
\Theta
extends in the usual way to
C^{\infty}(|\Omega|^{2})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(|\Omega|^{2})
by pulling-back. This set-up brings in another resolvant operator which is

C\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-1}.

(4.34)

Denote also by
\Pi_{+}:L^{2}(|\Omega|^{2})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
the orthogonal projection. Below we reprove lemma 3 of Jaffe and Ritter
[
38
]
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
|\Omega|^{2}
,
\Theta
,
\Pi_{+}
and
C
be as above. Then

\Pi_{+}\Theta C=\frac{1}{2}(C_{N}-C_{D})

(4.35)

on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
and
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

As in
[
38
]
, the key idea is the
method of images
, that is, with the help of reflection symmetry under
\Theta
, we have

C_{N}f=\Pi_{+}C(f+\Theta f),\quad\textrm{and}\quad C_{D}f=\Pi_{+}C(f-\Theta f),

(4.36)

for any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, since the functions (potentials)
C(f+\Theta f)
and
C(f-\Theta f)
satisfy, automatically and respectively, the Neumann and Dirichlet conditions along
\partial\Omega
(note, first, that
[\Theta,C]=0
since
\Theta
is an isometry, and second, for
x\in\partial\Omega
,
\partial_{\nu}|_{x}
is the eigenvector of eigenvalue
-1
of
\mathrm{d}\Theta_{x}
on
T_{x}|\Omega|^{2}
). We obtain the result immediately.
∎

In summary,

Corollary 4.14

(equivalent formulations of RP)
.

In the situation as above, we have

2\big\langle f,\Theta Cf\big\rangle_{L^{2}(|\Omega|^{2})}=\big\langle f,(C_{N}-C_{D})f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big\langle(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}f,(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}

(4.37)

for all
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, and all of the above quantities are nonnegative.
\Box

Remark 4.8
.

Note that the 3 quantities in (
4.37
) make sense respectively on the spaces
L^{2}(|\Omega|^{2})
,
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. While the first quantity is the original view of RP, the second quantity offers a
one-sided
view and the third provides a view
within the boundary

\partial\Omega
. Nevertheless, the map
\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
reflects the geometry of the bulk, see for example Paternain, Salo and Uhlmann
[
51
]
section 11.5.

Markov Property and Consequences

Materials in sections
5.1
,
5.2
and
5.4
are largely classical with an excellent source being Dimock
[
17
]
which we follow roughly. See also Simon
[
72
]
section III.3 and see Powell and Werner
[
55
]
section 4.2 for a probabilistic point of view.

Decompositions of Sobolev Spaces

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Recall from Appendix
C.1
the definitions of the spaces
W^{s}_{A}(M)
and
W^{s}_{U}(M)
for
A\subset M
closed and
U\subset M
open. Recall also (lemma
C.1
) that we have the isometric isomorphism
(\Delta+m^{2}):W^{1}(M)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}W^{-1}(M)
as we endow
W^{1}(M)
and
W^{-1}(M)
respectively with the inner products
\langle-,(\Delta+m^{2})-\rangle_{L^{2}}
and
\langle-,(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}-\rangle_{L^{2}}
.

Lemma 5.1

(cf.
[
17
]
section 2)
.

Let
A\subset M
be a closed set. Then
W^{1}(M)
and
W^{-1}(M)
decompose orthogonally as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}_{A}(M)^{\perp},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M\setminus A}^{\perp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M\setminus A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}^{\perp}}

(5.1)

which is
preserved
by the isometric isomorphism
\Delta+m^{2}
. In particular, we have

P_{A}(\Delta+m^{2})=(\Delta+m^{2})p_{M\setminus A}^{\perp},\quad\textrm{and}\quad P_{A}^{\perp}(\Delta+m^{2})=(\Delta+m^{2})p_{M\setminus A},

(5.2)

where
p_{M\setminus A}^{\perp}
,
p_{M\setminus A}
,
P_{A}
, and
P_{A}^{\perp}
are the corresponding orthogonal projections as indicated in the diagram.

Proof.

We just need to show that the image of
W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)
under
\Delta+m^{2}
is precisely
W^{-1}_{A}(M)^{\perp}
. Indeed, by our definition of the inner products we have

\langle(\Delta+m^{2})u,v\rangle_{W^{-1}(M)}=\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

(5.3)

for all
u\in W^{1}(M)
,
v\in W^{-1}(M)
, where the RHS denotes the duality (distributional) pairing. However, the
annihilator
of
W^{-1}_{A}(M)
under the duality pairing is exactly
W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)
, see lemma
C.3
. This translates as

\big\{u\in W^{1}(M)\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \langle(\Delta+m^{2})u,v\rangle_{W^{-1}(M)}=0\textrm{ for all }v\in W^{-1}_{A}(M)\big\}=W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M),

(5.4)

which is what we desired.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

(
5.1
) and (
5.2
) holds for
A=\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface (codimension one submanifold).
\Box

Indeed, the crucial relation (
5.3
) together with (
5.2
) gives

Corollary 5.3

(adjoints)
.

We have

\big\langle p_{M\setminus A}^{\perp}u,v\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=\big\langle u,P_{A}v\big\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\big\langle p_{M\setminus A}u,v\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=\big\langle u,P_{A}^{\perp}v\big\rangle_{L^{2}},

(5.5)

for
u\in W^{1}(M)
and
v\in W^{-1}(M)
.
\Box

Remark 5.1
.

Denote
U:=M\setminus A
. Recall from (
C.4
) that
W^{-1}(U):=W^{-1}_{A}(M)^{\perp}
. In fact, corollary
5.3
shows
P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}
coincides with the restriction map
\rho_{M|U}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(U)
, since if already
u\in W^{1}_{U}(M)
then
p_{U}u=i_{*}u
,
i_{*}:W^{1}_{U}(M)\longrightarrow W^{1}(M)
being inclusion.

We are thus lead naturally to the following and eventually corollary
5.5
.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
U\subset M
be an open set and
F\subset U
be a closed set. Then
W^{1}_{U}(M)
and
W^{-1}(U)
decompose further as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{U}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{U\setminus F}(M)^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{U\setminus F}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}(W^{-1}_{F}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}_{F}(M)^{\perp},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta+m^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(5.6)

where the orthogonal complements are taken respectively
inside

W^{1}_{U}(M)
and
W^{-1}(U)
, which is
preserved
by the isometric isomorphism
\Delta+m^{2}
. Also, we have commutation relations similar to (
5.2
).

Proof.

The only point needing explanation is

(\Delta+m^{2})(W^{1}_{U\setminus F}(M)^{\perp}\cap W_{U}^{1}(M))=P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}(W^{-1}_{F}(M)).

(5.7)

Indeed, since
\Delta+m^{2}
is bijective, by (
5.1
) we have

LHS

\displaystyle=W^{-1}_{F\cup(M\setminus U)}(M)\cap W_{M\setminus U}^{-1}(M)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(W^{-1}_{M\setminus U}(M)\oplus W^{-1}_{F}(M))\cap W_{M\setminus U}^{-1}(M)^{\perp}

(
F
and
M\setminus U
disjoint)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS},

where the direct sum in the second line is non-orthogonal.
∎

Now let
(\Omega,g)
be compact with (smooth) boundary, smoothly and isometrically embedded in closed
M
(if
\Omega
is a
real tunnelling geometry
, then one choice for
M
is the “isometric double”, see remark
4.7
) and
\Sigma
be embedded in
\Omega^{\circ}
. Then in particular

Corollary 5.5
.

Corollary
5.4
holds for
U=\Omega^{\circ}
and
\Sigma\subset\Omega^{\circ}
an embedded closed hypersurface.
\Box

The Markov Stochastic Decomposition of GFF

Again suppose
(M,g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface with induced metric. A probabilistic point of view of the results in this section is provided in Powell and Werner
[
55
]
section 4.1.

Lemma 5.6
.

We have

p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}=\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}\circ\tau_{\Sigma}

(5.8)

on
W^{1}(M)
, where
p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}:W^{1}(M)\longrightarrow W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection as in lemma
5.1
.

Proof.

We show that for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}f
solves the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})u=0,&\textrm{in }M\setminus\Sigma,\\
u|_{\Sigma}=\tau_{\Sigma}f,&\textrm{on }\Sigma.\end{array}\right.

(5.9)

Indeed, the first condition holds since
\supp((\Delta+m^{2})p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}f)\subset\Sigma
by lemma
5.1
. To show the second, notice
\tau_{\Sigma}=0
on
W_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{1}(M)
by its definition (
C.3
). Hence

\tau_{\Sigma}p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}=\tau_{\Sigma}(\mathbbm{1}-p_{M\setminus\Sigma})=\tau_{\Sigma},

(5.10)

on
W^{1}(M)
. We conclude the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
follows
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. Then there is a stochastic decomposition

\phi=\widehat{p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}}\phi+\widehat{p_{M\setminus\Sigma}}\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D},

(5.11)

into independent random fields
\phi_{\Sigma}
and
\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
. More precisely, for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
we define the random variables

\phi_{\Sigma}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{\Sigma}f),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{\Sigma}^{\perp}f).

(5.12)

Then,
\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
follows the law of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}
, while
\phi_{\Sigma}
solves the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})\phi_{\Sigma}=0,&\textrm{in }M\setminus\Sigma,\\
\phi_{\Sigma}|_{\Sigma}=\tau_{\Sigma}\phi,&\textrm{on }\Sigma,\end{array}\right.

(5.13)

almost surely.

Remark 5.2
.

The term “stochastic decomposition” is borrowed from Bogachev
[
13
]
remark 3.7.7.

Proof.

Firstly,
\phi_{\Sigma}
and
\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
are independent since by (
5.12
) their Gaussian Hilbert spaces are respectively
W_{\Sigma}^{-1}(M)
and
W_{\Sigma}^{-1}(M)^{\perp}=W^{-1}(M\setminus\Sigma)
. The latter also means
\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
follows
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}
. The last fact about
\phi_{\Sigma}
follows from lemma
5.6
and the uniqueness of the measurable linear extension (proposition
B.7
).
∎

In the same vein using corollary
5.5
, we have also a decomposition for the Dirichlet GFF on a domain
\Omega
with smooth boundary, with respect to a hypersurface
\Sigma
(isometrically) embedded in the interior
\Omega^{\circ}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose
\phi_{\Omega}^{D}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
follows
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
. Then there is a stochastic decomposition

\phi=\widehat{p_{\Omega^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}}\phi+\widehat{p_{\Omega^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma}}\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}+\phi_{\Omega\setminus\Sigma}^{D},

(5.14)

into independent random fields
(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}
and
\phi_{\Omega\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
. More precisely, for
f\in W^{-1}(\Omega^{\circ})
we define the random variables

(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\Omega}^{D}(P_{\Sigma}f),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\phi_{\Omega\setminus\Sigma}^{D}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\Omega}^{D}(P_{\Sigma}^{\perp}f).

(5.15)

Then,
\phi_{\Omega\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
follows the law of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega\setminus\Sigma,D}
, while
(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}
solves the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}=0,&\textrm{in }\Omega^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma,\\
(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}|_{\Sigma}=\tau_{\Sigma}\phi_{\Omega}^{D},&\textrm{on }\Sigma,\\
(\phi_{\Omega}^{D})_{\Sigma}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,&\textrm{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(5.16)

almost surely.
\Box

The following version of proposition
5.7
in the case of a dissecting (smooth isometrically embedded) hypersurface
\Sigma\subset M
such that
M\setminus\Sigma=M^{\circ}_{+}\sqcup M^{\circ}_{-}
, will be useful in definition
5.4
.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have
W^{-1}_{M_{-}}(M)^{\perp}\subset W^{-1}_{M_{+}}(M)
and thus
W^{-1}_{M_{+}}(M)=W^{-1}_{M_{-}}(M)^{\perp}\oplus W^{-1}_{\Sigma}(M)
. Similarly
W^{-1}_{M_{-}}(M)=W^{-1}_{M_{+}}(M)^{\perp}\oplus W^{-1}_{\Sigma}(M)
.

Proof.

By lemma
5.1
with
A=M_{-}
we see that
W^{-1}_{M_{-}}(M)^{\perp}=(\Delta+m^{2})(W^{1}_{M_{+}^{\circ}}(M))
. These distributions are supported in
M_{+}
since
\Delta+m^{2}
is local.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
follows
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. Then there is a stochastic decomposition

\phi=\widehat{p_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{\perp}}\phi+\widehat{p_{M_{+}^{\circ}}}\phi+\widehat{p_{M_{-}^{\circ}}}\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M_{+}^{\circ}}^{D}+\phi_{M_{-}^{\circ}}^{D},

(5.17)

into independent random fields. More precisely, for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
the random variables are defined by

\phi_{\Sigma}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{\Sigma}f),\quad\phi_{M_{+}^{\circ}}^{D}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{M_{-}}^{\perp}f),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\phi_{M_{-}^{\circ}}^{D}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{M_{+}}^{\perp}f),

(5.18)

and with
\phi_{\Sigma}
the same as in proposition
5.7
,
\phi_{M_{+}^{\circ}}^{D}
and
\phi_{M_{-}^{\circ}}^{D}
follows respectively
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M_{+}^{\circ},D}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M_{-}^{\circ},D}
. Moreover,
(\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M_{+}^{\circ}}^{D})(f)=\phi(P_{M_{+}}f)
,
(\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M_{-}^{\circ}}^{D})(f)=\phi(P_{M_{-}}f)
.
\Box

The Bayes Principle Applied to GFF

Let now
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}\subset M

non-intersecting
isometrically embedded smooth closed hypersurfaces. The goal of this section is to derive the Bayes principle relating the probability laws of the two random fields
\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi
and
\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi
where
\phi
is the GFF on
M
. To avoid convolving with nomenclatures of conditional probabilities we prefer a direct measure theoretic argument, although these are clearly equivalent. Throughout this section we identify continuous linear maps of Cameron-Martin spaces with their measurable extensions to distributional
Q
-spaces, as well as their induced actions on measures.

To begin with, by proposition
5.7
we have a stochastic decomposition

\phi=\phi_{\Sigma_{1}}+\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{D}

(5.19)

for
\phi\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, the two components
independent
of each other. Then, apply
\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}
we get

\displaystyle\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi

\displaystyle=\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi_{\Sigma_{1}}+\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{D}=\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi+\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{D}

(5.20)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi+\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{D}.

(5.21)

Here we define

\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{1})&\longrightarrow&C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{2}),\\
W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{1})&\longrightarrow&W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{2}),\end{array}\right.

(5.22)

called the
transition operator/propagator
. Define accordingly

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{G}_{M,2}^{1}:W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{1})\times W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{2})&\longrightarrow&W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{1})\times W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{2}),\\
(\varphi_{1},h)&\longmapsto&(\varphi_{1},h+\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1}).\end{array}\right.

(5.23)

called the
graph operator
.

Lemma 5.11
.

We have

\tau_{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})=(\mathcal{G}_{M,2}^{1})_{*}(\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}\otimes\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}),

(5.24)

where
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}
and
\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}
are Gaussian measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{2})
with covariances
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}})^{-1}
and
(\DN_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},D})^{-1}
, respectively.

Proof.

Following (
5.21
),
\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{D}=\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi-\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi
is independent of
\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi
, and follows the law
\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}
, while
\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}\phi
follows the law
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}
, both by proposition
4.9
.
∎

We could also define the operators
\mathcal{M}_{M,1}^{2}
and
\mathcal{G}_{M,1}^{2}
with the roles of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
switched. Note that lemma
5.11
is entirely symmetric under the switching of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
. Recall the measures
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
from corollary
2.15
.

Proposition 5.12

(Bayes Principle for GFF)
.

Let now
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}\subset M
non-intersecting isometrically embedded smooth closed hypersurfaces. We have equality of Radon-Nikodym densities

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi_{2})

(5.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},M}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi_{2})\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{M,1}^{2}\varphi_{2})_{*}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{1},M\setminus\Sigma_{2}}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1}).

(5.26)

Here
(\varphi)_{*}
denotes the shift induced by
\varphi
on measures as in corollary
2.5
.

Proof.

We just need to prove (
5.25
). In other words,
(\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}
is the conditional law of
\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\phi
provided “
\tau_{\Sigma_{1}}=\varphi_{1}
”. The proof is straightforward. For positive (Borel) measurable functionals
F\in L^{+}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1}))
,
G\in L^{+}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{2}))
, we have by lemma
5.11
, the change of variables formula, and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\int(F\otimes G)(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})

\displaystyle=\iint(F\otimes G)\circ\mathcal{G}_{M,2}^{1}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle=\int F(\varphi_{1})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}(\varphi_{1})\int G(\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\int F(\varphi_{1})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},M}\int G(\varphi_{2})\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{\DN,D}^{\Sigma_{2},M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}.

as
F
and
G
range over all positive measurable functionals, we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Applying corollary
2.15
and the Cameron-Martin formula (
2.12
), one obtains a relation between quadratic forms (the corresponding relation for determinants also works out by BFK),

\displaystyle\quad\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi_1}{x},\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi_1}{x}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\left\langle\varphi_{1},\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}\varphi_{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{1})}+\left\langle x,\DN_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},D}x\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{2})}

\displaystyle\quad-2\left\langle x,\DN_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},D}\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{2})}+\left\langle\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1},\DN_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},D}\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{2})}

which, of course, can also be obtained directly noting

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi_{1},\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}\varphi_{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{1})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi_1}{\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^1\varphi_1},\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi_1}{\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^1\varphi_1}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2})},

\displaystyle\left\langle x,\DN_{M\setminus\Sigma_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},D}x\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{2})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{0}{x},\DN_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{0}{x}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2})},

et cetera, and expanding the left hand side by writing
[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
x\end{smallmatrix}]=[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1}\end{smallmatrix}]+[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
x-\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}\varphi_{1}\end{smallmatrix}]
.

Remark 5.4
.

In the case
M=\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
with
\Sigma_{1}:=\Sigma\times\{0\}
,
\Sigma_{2}:=\Sigma\times\{t\}
, and Dirichlet condition at
\Sigma\times\{\pm\infty\}
(namely decay at
\infty
), we have the explicit expression

\mathcal{M}^{1}_{M,2}=\textrm{e}^{-t\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}},

(5.27)

where
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}=(\Delta_{\Sigma}+m^{2})^{1/2}
. Also, in this case
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}=\DN_{M_{+}}^{\Sigma}
,
M_{+}=\Sigma\times[0,+\infty)
.

Nelson’s Markov Property and Reflection Positivity

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold. We consider a family of
\sigma
-algebras on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
generated by the GFF random variables. Recall that for all
f\in W^{-1}(M)
, we have defined
\phi\longmapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
as a random variable. Now for
A\subset M
closed, we define the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{O}_{A}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sigma\left(\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in W_{A}^{-1}(M)\right).

(5.28)

Note since
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
is a separable Fréchet space, its Borel
\sigma
-algebra is generated by
\phi(f)
for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. The GFF
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{M}
is thus finer than the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

Remark 5.5
.

What we discuss here is only a preliminary consideration based on the GFF. The theory of Markov random fields is a well-developed subject and we refer to the monograph of Rozanov
[
62
]
for thorough treatment. See also the interesting result of Gu and Mourrat
[
30
]
theorem 2.1, as well as the comments thereafter.

Definition 5.1

(
[
72
]
theorem III.9)
.

A probability measure
\mu
on
(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O})
has the
domain Markov property
if for all closed sets
A
,
B\subset M
such that
A^{\circ}\cap B=\varnothing
, and all
F\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O},\mu)
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{B}]\penalty\ |\mathcal{O}_{A}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{B}]\penalty\ |\mathcal{O}_{\partial A}].

(5.29)

In other words, for all
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-measurable
L^{2}
-functional
F
we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{A}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\partial A}]
.

Proposition 5.13

(
[
17
]
theorem 1)
.

The measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
has the domain Markov property.

Proof.

For details see Simon
[
72
]
section III.3 and Dimock
[
17
]
. The crucial point being that by
locality
of the
W^{1}
-inner product we have
C_{c}^{\infty}(A^{\circ})\subset W^{1}_{M\setminus A}(M)^{\perp}
. Therefore by corollary
5.3
if
A^{\circ}\cap B=\varnothing
and
v\in W^{-1}_{B}(M)
,
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(A^{\circ})
then

\big\langle P_{A}v,u\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=\big\langle v,p_{M\setminus A}^{\perp}u\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=\big\langle v,u\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

(5.30)

This shows
P_{A}P_{B}=P_{\partial A}P_{B}
. The result then follows by applying the Itô-Wiener-Segal isomorphism.
∎

Suppose we have a dissecting (smooth isometrically embedded) hypersurface
\Sigma\subset M
such that
M\setminus\Sigma=M^{\circ}_{+}\sqcup M^{\circ}_{-}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let the probability measure
\mu
on
(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O})
have the domain Markov property. If real functionals
F\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{M_{+}},\mu)
,
G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{M_{-}},\mu)
are such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[G|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]
almost surely, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[FG]\geqslant 0.

(5.31)

Proof.

The closed sets
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
are such that
M_{+}^{\circ}\cap M_{-}=\varnothing
. Also
\partial M_{+}=\partial M_{-}=\Sigma
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[FG]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ F\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[G|\mathcal{O}_{M_{+}}]\penalty\ ]

(
F
is
\mathcal{O}_{M_{+}}
-measurable)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ F\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[G|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]\penalty\ ]

(domain Markov and
G
is
\mathcal{O}_{M_{-}}
-measurable)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[G|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]\penalty\ ]\geqslant 0,

(
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[G|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}]
is
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}
-measurable)

by our assumption.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Because of proposition
5.7
, for GFF the conditional expectation represents “integrating out redundant degrees of freedom”. Indeed, following proposition
5.7
we write
F(\phi)
as
F(\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D})
then we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}](\phi_{\Sigma})=\int F(\phi_{\Sigma},\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}(\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}).

(5.32)

See Simon
[
72
]
page 225 (in particular equation (VII.26)) for details.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
j_{\Sigma}
be the map in lemma
4.5
and let
W^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\DN}(\Sigma)
denote the Gaussian Hilbert space of
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}
, as in lemma
2.4
. Then

j_{\Sigma}:W^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\DN}(\Sigma)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}W^{-1}_{\Sigma}(M)

(5.33)

is an isometric isomorphism. In particular,
W_{\Sigma}^{-1}(M)\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

By lemma
5.1
, lemma
5.6
and lemma
4.6
,
j_{\Sigma}
is a surjective isometry.
∎

Suppose we are in the situation of remark
4.7
. Here we take
M=|\Omega|^{2}
. The isometry
\Theta
acts on
\mathcal{O}
-measurable functionals
F:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(|\Omega|^{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
\Theta^{*}F(\phi):=F(\Theta\phi)
and
\Theta\phi(f):=\phi(\Theta^{*}f)
. The action of
\Theta^{*}
on
C^{\infty}(|\Omega|^{2})
extends unitarily to
W^{-1}(|\Omega|^{2})
, since
\Theta
is isometry on
|\Omega|^{2}
(commutes with Laplacian).

Definition 5.2
.

A probability measure
\mu
on
(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(|\Omega|^{2}),\mathcal{O})
is called
reflection positive
if for all real functionals
F\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(|\Omega|^{2}),\mathcal{O}_{\Omega},\mu)
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F(\Theta F)]\geqslant 0.

(5.34)

Proposition 5.16

(
[
17
]
theorem 2)
.

The measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is reflection positive.

Proof.

We first claim
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\Theta F|\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}]
almost surely. For GFF this is seen via the Itô-Wiener-Segal isomorphism. Indeed the action of
\Theta^{*}
descends to
\Theta^{*}F(\phi)=\phi(\Theta^{*}f_{1})\cdots\phi(\Theta^{*}f_{k})
if
F(\phi)=\phi(f_{1})\cdots\phi(f_{k})
. Thus
\Theta^{*}=\Gamma(\Theta^{*}|_{W^{-1}})
, implying also that

\Theta^{*}:L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{\Omega},\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{\Omega^{*}},\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}).

(5.35)

Since
\Theta
fixes
\partial\Omega
we have that
\Theta^{*}
is the identity on
W^{-1}_{\partial\Omega}
. Since
\Theta^{*}
is also
W^{-1}
-unitary we eventually have
P_{\partial\Omega}\Theta^{*}=\Theta^{*}P_{\Theta\partial\Omega}=\Theta^{*}P_{\partial\Omega}=P_{\partial\Omega}
, and the claim is true. The proposition then follows from (
5.35
), proposition
5.13
and lemma
5.14
.
∎

Following proposition
5.16
we obtain a nonnegative bilinear form on
L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{\Omega},\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
:

(F,G)\longmapsto\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F(\Theta G)].

(5.36)

We define
\mathcal{N}:=\{F\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F(\Theta F)]=0\}
, namely the kernel of (
5.36
). We use shorthand notations

L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{\Omega},\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{E}_{+},\quad\quad L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega},\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}.

Proposition 5.17
.

\overline{\mathcal{E}_{+}/\mathcal{N}}\cong\Gamma(j_{\partial\Omega})L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega),\mu_{\DN}^{\partial\Omega,M})
, where the closure is taken under (
5.36
).

Proof.

Indeed, by the proofs of lemma
5.14
and proposition
5.16
we see that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F(\Theta G)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}]\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[G|\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}]\penalty\ ]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\Gamma(P_{\partial\Omega})F\cdot\Gamma(P_{\partial\Omega})G]

(5.37)

for
F
,
G\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
. Note
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}\subset\mathcal{E}_{+}
and thus
\mathcal{N}=\ker\Gamma(P_{\partial\Omega})
showing
\overline{\mathcal{E}_{+}/\mathcal{N}}\cong\overline{\ran\Gamma(P_{\partial\Omega})}=\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}
. But we know
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}=\Gamma(W^{-1}_{\partial\Omega}(M))
therefore we obtain our result by lemma
5.15
and Itô-Wiener-Segal.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Functionals in
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}
are usually called
sharp-time functionals
(see Jaffe and Ritter
[
39
]
) and
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\Omega}
is usually taken as the quantum mechanical
Hilbert space
over the “time-zero slice”
\partial\Omega
. This Hilbert space is isomorphic to the one considered in section
6.2
, conjugated by the square root of the Radon-Nikodym density between
\mu_{\DN}^{\partial\Omega,M}
and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\partial\Omega}
.

Remark 5.8
.

It is reasonable that a measure defined on
(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(|\Omega|^{2}),\mathcal{O})
satisfying the domain Markov property and that is
\Theta
-invariant
will be reflection positive. Here we define
\Theta
-invariant as such that
\Theta^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[-|\mathcal{O}_{\Theta A}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[-|\mathcal{O}_{A}]
for all closed sets
A\subset|\Omega|^{2}
. Indeed, since
\Theta
maps level sets of
\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
to level sets of
\left\langle-,\Theta^{*}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
(by
L^{2}
-unitarity of
\Theta
),
\Theta\mathcal{O}_{A}=\mathcal{O}_{\Theta A}
via its action on indicator functionals. In this sense one shows that
\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}
is invariant under
\Theta
and further that
\mathcal{O}_{\partial\Omega}
-measurable functionals are also invariant, via approximating by simple functionals. This together with lemma
5.14
gives reflection positivity.

Remark 5.9
.

Comparing proposition
4.10
and corollary
4.14
we recover the covariance structure of the induced field
\PI^{\partial\Omega}_{\Omega}\varphi
for
\varphi\sim\mu_{2\DN}^{\partial\Omega,\Omega}
with covariance
\frac{1}{2}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}
to be

\mathbb{E}\big[\PI^{\partial\Omega}_{\Omega}\varphi(f)\PI^{\partial\Omega}_{\Omega}\varphi(h)\big]=\big\langle P_{\partial\Omega}f,P_{\partial\Omega}h\big\rangle_{W^{-1}(|\Omega|^{2})}

(5.38)

for
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. This coheres with (
5.37
) for
F=\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
and
G=\left\langle-,h\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
.

Locality of the
P(\phi)
interaction and the Restricted GFF

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian surface and
\Omega^{\circ}\subset M
a domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. Denote
\Omega=\overline{\Omega^{\circ}}
.

Remark 5.10
.

In general one could consider
regular
domains
\Omega^{\circ}
. A domain is called
regular
if
W^{-1}_{\Omega^{\circ}}(M)=W^{-1}_{\Omega}(M)
, the two spaces defined by (
C.3
) and (
C.2
). See also Simon
[
72
]
page 267.

Suppose
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
. We define

S_{\Omega,\varepsilon,\chi}(\phi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi_{\Omega,\varepsilon}^{D}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega,\varepsilon}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x),

(5.39)

where

\phi_{\Omega,\varepsilon}^{D}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{\overline{\Omega^{c}}}^{\perp}E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}),\quad\phi_{\partial\Omega,\varepsilon}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi(P_{\partial\Omega}E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}),\quad\phi\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}.

(5.40)

Proposition 5.18

(locality)
.

Whenever
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, we have, for any fixed Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}
,

S_{M,\chi}(\phi)=\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}S_{\Omega,\varepsilon,\chi}(\phi),

(5.41)

and hence
S_{M,\chi}
is
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
-measurable. In particular,

\int_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}=\int_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}

(5.42)

and is
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
-measurable.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we suppose
\supp\chi
stays
\delta
-away from
\partial\Omega
for some
\delta>0
. Then corollary
3.9
and approximation in
L^{4}
of a general
\chi
as well as
1_{\Omega}
gives the result. We note that the support of
E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}
is contained in the
\varepsilon
-ball around
x
. Then whenever
\varepsilon<\delta
for
x\in\supp\chi
we have
\supp(E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x})\subset\Omega
, hence

P_{\Omega}^{\perp}E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}=0,

(5.43)

and

\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\equiv\phi(P_{\overline{\Omega^{c}}}^{\perp}E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x})+\phi(P_{\partial\Omega}E_{\varepsilon}\delta_{x}),

(5.44)

both
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
-measurable. Thus

{:}P(\phi_{\Omega,\varepsilon}^{D}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega,\varepsilon}(x)){:}\equiv{:}P(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)){:}

(5.45)

for
x\in\supp\chi
and
\varepsilon<\delta
, and is
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
-measurable. Therefore (
5.41
) holds by proposition
3.5
.
∎

We emphasize here that for our purposes we employ the Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}_{0}
of section
3.4
in defining
S_{M,\chi}
. This is to say

{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{j}(2n)!}{(2n-2j)!j!2^{j}}C_{\varepsilon}(x)^{j}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n-2j},

(5.46)

where

C_{\varepsilon}(x)=\iint E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)C_{0}(y,z)E_{\varepsilon}(z,x)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z).

(5.47)

Since
E_{\varepsilon}(x,-)
is supported in an
\varepsilon
-ball around
x
, and
C_{0}(y,z)
depends only on the geometry of
M
resctricted to a convex neighborhood of
y
,
z
, the term
C_{\varepsilon}(x)
depends only on the geometry of
M
locally near
x
. This means that under
{:}\bullet{:}_{0}
, once
\supp\chi\subset\Omega
, the limiting (integrated) random variable
S_{M,\chi}
, in addition to being
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
-measurable, is in fact fully determined with knowledge of the metric
g|_{\Omega}
restricted to
\Omega
. This allows the freedom of choosing the ambient manifold
M
where
\Omega
isometrically embeds in defining the interaction over
\Omega
(see definition
5.4
).

Now we repeat proposition
5.18
in a different way:

Corollary 5.19
.

Whenever
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, for any fixed Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[S_{M,\chi}|\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}]=S_{M,\chi},

(5.48)

and
S_{M,\chi}
is independent of
\phi_{\Omega^{c}}^{D}
, that is, of
\phi(P_{\Omega}^{\perp}f)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
or
\phi((\Delta+m^{2})f)
for all
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{c})
. Moreover, since
\Omega
is regular,
S_{M,\chi}
is the limit in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
of polynomials of random variables of the form
\phi(f)
with
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
via the Itô-Wiener-Segal isomorphism.
\Box

This result motivates

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\Omega^{\circ}\subset M
be a regular domain. The
(massive) Gaussian Free Field
over
\Omega^{\circ}

restricted from
M
is the centered Gaussian process indexed by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, with covariance

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,h\right\rangle_{W^{-1}_{\Omega}(M)}

(5.49)

for any
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. We denote it by
\phi|_{\Omega}
.

Since the inner product of
W^{-1}_{\Omega}(M)
depends on
M
, the restricted GFF does not make sense on
\Omega^{\circ}
alone. However, by corollary
5.10
, it is equal in law to
\phi_{\partial\Omega}+\phi_{\Omega}^{D}
, where
\phi_{\Omega}^{D}
does make sense over
\Omega^{\circ}
and the law of
\phi_{\partial\Omega}=\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}(\tau_{\partial\Omega}\phi)
is determined via
\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}
by that of a boundary data over
\partial\Omega
.

Going back to corollary
5.19
we see that for
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, the interaction
S_{M,\chi}
can now be defined as a random variable of the sample
\phi|_{\Omega}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
. Equally, it is also a random variable over
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega)
equipped with
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{C}^{\partial\Omega}
where
\mu_{C}^{\partial\Omega}
is some probability measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega)
mutually absolutely continuous with respect to
\tau_{\partial\Omega}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
and on the same
\sigma
-algebra. We consider only a few very specific candidates for
\mu_{C}^{\partial\Omega}
.

Now suppose we start with a Riemannian surface
(\Omega,g)
with totally geodesic boundary
\partial\Omega
. With the help of the isometric double
|\Omega|^{2}
discussed in remark
4.7
we define

Definition 5.4
.

The
interaction over
\Omega
is

S_{\Omega}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}|\varphi)=S_{\Omega}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}+\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}S_{|\Omega|^{2},1_{\Omega}}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}+\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}\varphi)

(5.50)

as an
L^{2}
-random variable over
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\partial\Omega)
equipped with
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{2\DN}^{\partial\Omega,\Omega}
, where the latter is the Gaussian measure with covariance operator
\frac{1}{2}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}
.

Remark 5.11
.

We emphasize here that the variable
S_{\Omega}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}|\varphi)
must be understood for
\phi_{\Omega}^{D}
and
\varphi

both
random, with the law of
\varphi
mutually absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{2\DN}^{\partial\Omega,\Omega}
(thus
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\partial\Omega}
is a valid candidate). Thus if one asks how much can one “fix”
\varphi
as one interprets
S_{\Omega}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}|\varphi)
, the answer is that it makes sense as a random variable of
\phi_{\Omega}^{D}
alone for almost every fixed
\varphi
under
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\partial\Omega}
(but this full-measure set with respect to
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\partial\Omega}
is generally unknown). Said from a slightly different perspective, the expression
S_{\Omega}(\phi)
for a generic
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
makes sense as a random variable only when
\phi
follows a law mutually absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}*(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})_{*}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\partial\Omega}
, where “
*
” denotes convolution product. Statements involving
S_{\Omega}(\phi_{\Omega}^{D}|\varphi)
in the sequel should be understood in this sense.

The following result will be useful in lemma
6.2
.

Proposition 5.20
.

For any fixed Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}
we have

\int_{M}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}=\int_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}+\int_{\Omega^{c}}{:}P(\phi_{\Omega^{c}}^{D}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega^{c}}

(5.51)

in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
and in particular pointwise almost surely, as a result

\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}}=\textrm{e}^{-\int_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{\Omega^{c}}{:}P(\phi_{\Omega^{c}}^{D}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega^{c}}}

(5.52)

pointwise almost surely and thus also in
L^{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
.

Remark 5.12
.

Note that the equality (
5.51
) says nothing about pointwise almost sure convergence of the mollified sequences
S_{M,\varepsilon}
,
S_{\Omega,\varepsilon}
, and
S_{\Omega^{c},\varepsilon}
. In fact, only separate subsequences in
\varepsilon
will converge pointwise almost surely to the three respective terms in (
5.51
).

Proof.

Consider a smooth partition of unity

1_{M}=\chi_{\Omega}+\chi_{\partial\Omega}+\chi_{\Omega^{c}},

(5.53)

where
\chi_{\Omega}
and
\chi_{\Omega^{c}}
are supported in the interiors of
\Omega
and
\Omega^{c}
respectively and
\chi_{\partial\Omega}
is supported near
\partial\Omega
. Note then that (
5.53
) holds as well in
L^{4}(M)
so by corollary
3.9
we have

\int_{M}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}=\int_{M}\chi_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}+\int_{M}\chi_{\partial\Omega}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}+\int_{M}\chi_{\Omega^{c}}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}

(5.54)

in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
for
1\leqslant p<\infty
. Now take

\chi_{\Omega}\to 1_{\Omega},\quad\chi_{\Omega^{c}}\to 1_{\Omega^{c}},\quad\textrm{and }\chi_{\partial\Omega}\to 0,

(5.55)

in
L^{4}(M)
, keeping the equality (
5.53
) in the process. Then

\displaystyle\int_{M}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}1_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}+\int_{M}1_{\Omega^{c}}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}{:}P(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega}+\int_{\Omega^{c}}{:}P(\phi_{\Omega^{c}}^{D}(x)+\phi_{\partial\Omega}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{\Omega^{c}}

in
L^{p}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
for
1\leqslant p<\infty
, and we finish the proof.
∎

Presenting the
P(\phi)_{2}
Model as a Segal Theory

Description of Segal’s Rules as Pertains to the Model

The prototype of what we propose here is definition (4.4) on page 460 of
[
67
]
, then we relate to the “transfer operator” formalism on page 455-456 (lemma
6.1
). We will consider real (separable) Hilbert spaces. We denote the category of such spaces with Hilbert-Schmidt operators as morphisms by
\mathbf{Hilb}_{\mathbb{R}}
. The reason for Hilbert-Schmidt is lemma
A.7
.

As a preliminary consideration, let
\mathcal{C}
denote the “category” whose objects are finite disjoint unions
\Sigma_{j}=\bigsqcup_{i}\mathbb{S}^{1}_{i}
of Riemannian circles, each of which determined by its radius
R_{i}
, and for any finite collection of such objects
(\Sigma_{j})_{j}
we say that an
cobordism/unoriented morphism
among them is simply an orientable Riemannian surface with a
totally geodesic
boundary
\partial\Omega
which is identified with
\bigsqcup_{j}\Sigma_{j}
via an isometry (see also remark
6.4
). With this in mind we can consider the following definition. Morphisms will be oriented once we make the transfer operator connection.

Definition 6.1

(
[
67
]
page 460)
.
