A 2d Riemannian QFT is a correspondence
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Hilb}_{\mathbb{R}}
, such that

1.

to each oriented Riemannian circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}
of radius
R
there is associated a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{R}
, and to the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{S}_{i}^{1}
there is associated the tensor product
\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}
of the corresponding single-circle Hilbert spaces; in the degenerate case
R=0
, we let
\mathcal{H}_{0}=\mathbb{R}
;

2.

(
operator-reflection
) to each Riemannian surface
\Omega
such that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma
, without distinguishing the orientations on the components, we associate an element
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
; in the degenerate case where
\Omega=M
is closed, we associate a real number
Z_{M}\in\mathbb{R}
, called the
partition function
;

3.

(
sewing-trace
) suppose
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
are two connected components of
\Sigma
that are isometric, and let
\rho:\Sigma_{i}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{j}
be an (orientation reversing) isometry. Then

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}=\ttr_{\rho}(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}),

(6.1)

where
\Omega/\rho
is the surface obtained from
\Omega
by gluing
\Sigma_{i}
with
\Sigma_{j}
along
\rho
, and
\ttr_{\rho}
is the
trace map
such that writing
\Sigma=\Sigma_{i}\sqcup\Sigma_{j}\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}
(possibly
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
),
\ttr_{\rho}
is the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\ttr_{\rho}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}},\\
F\otimes G\otimes H&\longmapsto&\left\langle\rho_{*}F,G\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{j}}H,\end{array}\right.

(6.2)

with
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{\Sigma_{j}}
being the (real) inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}
.

Remark 6.1
.

A word of caution should be said immediately concerning (
6.2
). As written,
\ttr_{\rho}
would not extend continuously to the whole of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
for “infinitely entangled” states in
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}
. However, for us each
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
is
L^{2}(Q_{\Sigma},\mu_{\Sigma})
for a probability space
(Q_{\Sigma},\mu_{\Sigma})
, and thus by proposition
A.4
,
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
is represented as
L^{2}(Q\times Q\times Q^{\prime},\mu\otimes\mu\otimes\mu^{\prime})
, where we identify
(Q_{\Sigma_{i}},\mu_{\Sigma_{i}})
and
(Q_{\Sigma_{j}},\mu_{\Sigma_{j}})
with
(Q,\mu)
via the isometry. The action of
\ttr_{\rho}
should be understood as

\ttr_{\rho}:\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(x,y,z)\longmapsto\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(x,x,z)\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

(6.3)

when the latter integral converges, which we will show to happen for our model in sections
6.4
and
6.5
. This is analogous to the “flat trace” of Atiyah and Bott
[
3
]
. It can be shown easily that (
6.3
) coincides with (
6.2
) on “finitely entangled” states of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, that is, finite linear combinations
\sum_{i}F_{i}\otimes G_{i}\otimes H_{i}
. However, the finiteness of neither (
6.3
) nor (
6.2
) would imply the operator
U_{\Omega}
of (
6.4
) is trace class proper.

Now we manufacture Hilbert-Schmidt operators in
\mathbf{Hilb}_{\mathbb{R}}
out of definition
6.1
with the help of lemma
A.7
. Let
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
be two objects in
\mathcal{C}
, now
oriented
. We say that an unoriented morphism
\Omega
among
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is in
\Mor(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
if the isometry
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\longrightarrow\partial\Omega
identifies the orientation of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
with that induced by an
inward pointing
normal on
\partial\Omega
, and that of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
an
outward pointing
normal.

Lemma
A.7
and definition
6.1
now implies

Lemma 6.1
.

If
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
, then the
Segal transfer operator

U_{\Omega}
defined by

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}U_{\Omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}},\\
F&\longmapsto&\ttr_{\rho}(F\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}),\end{array}\right.

(6.4)

where
\ttr_{\rho}
is the map of (
6.3
) with
\Sigma_{i}=\Sigma_{j}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
, and
\rho=\mathbbm{1}
, is Hilbert-Schmidt. Moreover,

1.

if
\Omega_{1}\in\Mor(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
,
\Omega_{2}\in\Mor(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})
, and
\Omega_{2}\cup_{2}\Omega_{1}
the Riemannian connected sum by gluing components corresponding to
\Sigma_{2}
, then

U_{\Omega_{2}\cup_{2}\Omega_{1}}=U_{\Omega_{2}}\circ U_{\Omega_{1}}.

(6.5)

2.

suppose
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
, with
\Sigma
identified with itself along an isometry
\rho
, and denote by
\check{\Omega}
the closed surface obtained by gluing these components together, then

Z_{\check{\Omega}}=\ttr_{\rho}(U_{\Omega}).

(6.6)

3.

if
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
, denote by
\Omega^{*}\in\Mor(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{1})
the surface obtained by reversing the orientations of boundaries of
\Omega
without changing the orientation of
\Omega
, then

U_{\Omega^{*}}=U_{\Omega}^{\dagger},

(6.7)

with
U_{\Omega}^{\dagger}
denoting the (real) adjoint of
U_{\Omega}
.
\Box

Figure 1:
sewing

Remark 6.2
.

As alluded to at the bottom of subsection
2.2.3
, one could allow each
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}
to be a
ray
instead of a fixed vector in
\mathcal{H}_{\partial\Omega}
, which eventually enables an arbitrary (nonzero) constant to be included in (
6.5
), obtaining the so-called
projective
version of the axiom. This is reasonable since quantum states are rays, not vectors.

Remark 6.3
.

An interesting remark appears at the bottom of page 457 of
[
67
]
, namely the conjecture of Friedan that a theory in the sense of definition
6.1
is in fact completely determined by its restriction to
closed surfaces
, namely the knowledge of the numbers
Z_{M}
for all closed
M
. While far from proving this conjecture, we shall see in section
6.3
that the correct definition of the amplitude “derives” very naturally from a special case of the trace axiom ((iii) of definition
6.1
). It seems certain that if one has knowledge not just of the numbers
Z_{M}
but the
measures

\mu_{P(\phi)}^{M}
as in (
2.1
), then the theory would be determined.

Remark 6.4
.

To have safety of smooth gluing while taking into account at the same time our reflection constructions (the isometric double) required in section
6.3
, one should enhance the objects
\Sigma
with
symmetric two-sided collars

\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
equipped with symmetric metrics making
\Sigma\times\{0\}
geodesic, and a cobordism
\Omega
as described above must allow each
\Sigma_{j}
as a component of
\partial\Omega
to have a tubular neighborhood isometric to
\Sigma_{j}\times(-\varepsilon,0]
. Here
symmetric
means having an isometric involution
\Theta
that exchanges
\Sigma\times\{\pm t\}
and fixes exactly
\Sigma\times\{0\}
(actually the reflection would ensure
\Sigma\times\{0\}
being geodesic, see
[
59
]
section 2.1.1 and Alekseevsky et al
[
4
]
). In fact, one should consider all proper ingredients that make up what is called the
Riemannian bordism category
as described in detail in section 6.2 of H. Hohnhold, S. Stolz, and P. Teichner
[
36
]
. Perhaps a better consideration is not fixed collars but
germs
of collars; see also the discussion in Kontsevich and Segal
[
41
]
following definition 3.1. However, the problem of finding the right underlying category is somewhat orthogonal to the problems considered in this article.

The Hilbert Spaces

Now we associate a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{R}
to the Riemannian circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}
of radius
R
. Let

\mathbf{D}_{R}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}}+m^{2})^{1/2}

(6.8)

be the positive square root of the positive Helmholtz operator on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}
. The circle having radius
R
would mean that we take
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}}:=-\partial_{\theta}^{2}
when the metric is
R\mathrm{d}\theta
, parametrized by the arc length
\theta
. Denote by
\mu_{(2\mathbf{D}_{R})^{-1}}
the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R})
with covariance
(2\mathbf{D}_{R})^{-1}
(for convenience, we just write
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}
). Then we define

\mathcal{H}_{R}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{R}),\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}).

(6.9)

Here we take
\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}:=\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}\cdot\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{R})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, namely the probability measure scaled by the positive finite constant
\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{R})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Indeed, this same method could be applied directly to (finite) disjoint unions
\Sigma=\bigsqcup_{i}\mathbb{S}_{i}^{1}
and produce a measure on the corresponding
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
, and this is compatible with (i) of definition
6.1
. Indeed,

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{1}\sqcup\mathbb{S}^{1}_{2})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{1})\oplus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{2})

(6.10)

and the operator
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
acts diagonally, and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\mathbb{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathbb{S}_{2}}=\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\mathbb{S}_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\mathbb{S}_{2}}
in view of lemma
2.7
(for Gaussian fields). The real
L^{2}
space would then be the tensor product (lemma
A.4
). Alternatively, one could argue from
B.5
.

Amplitudes = Schwartz Kernels

Let
\Omega
be an unoriented morphism among
(\Sigma_{j})_{j}
. We denote
\Sigma:=\bigsqcup_{j}\Sigma_{j}
, and seek to define
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
. We consider gluing
\Omega
with itself along an orientation reversing isometry
\rho:\Sigma\longrightarrow\Sigma
, forming the isometric double
|\Omega|^{2}=\Omega^{*}\cup_{\Sigma}\Omega
(here
\Omega^{*}
, as in (iii) of lemma
6.1
, denote the copy of
\Omega
with coorientation of
\Sigma
reversed). Suppose we have defined
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}
satisfying definition
6.1
, then (iii) would imply

\displaystyle Z_{|\Omega|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\varphi)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)}\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big)(\varphi)\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

In the case
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
this corresponds to the fact that
Z_{|\Omega|^{2}}=\ttr_{\rho}(U_{\Omega^{*}}U_{\Omega})
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\Omega
be an unoriented morphism among
\Sigma
and
P
the polynomial defining the interaction. We define the
amplitude
associated to
\Omega
to be the quantity

\mathcal{A}^{P}_{\Omega}(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigg[\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}\big[\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big)\big]}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}},

(6.11)

where the second ratio denotes the Radon-Nikodym density. (Compare Pickrell
[
53
]
definition 3.)

This is well-defined since
\tau_{\Sigma}(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})\ll\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
as
\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega}\ll\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
by corollary
2.15
, and both are finite positive measures. We see also that
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}>0
almost surely with respect to
\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. We have automatically
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma})=\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
since
|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}|^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma})
by definition.

Example 6.1
.

Let us derive the amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}
for the free field, that is, with
S_{|\Omega|^{2}}=0
. Indeed,

|\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\Omega}(\varphi)|^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}\detz(2\mathbf{D})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}(\varphi).

(6.12)

Since

\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega},

(6.13)

namely the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
\frac{1}{2}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
, taking into account the BFK formula

\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})=[\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})]^{2}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma}),

(6.14)

we obtain, by corollary
2.15
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)=\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi,(\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma}-\mathbf{D})\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}}.

(6.15)

Lemma 6.2
.

We have

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(6.16)

for almost every
\varphi
under
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. Here
S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)
is as defined in definition
5.4
.

Proof.

Indeed, by definition of the measure image,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi)^{2}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)^{2}\iint\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}+\PI_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}).

(6.17)

However, by proposition
5.20
which decouples the interaction into a sum over complementary regions,

\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)}=\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega^{*}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}|\varphi)},

(6.18)

almost surely againt
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}\otimes\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})
, and hence also against
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. Thus by reflection symmetry between
\Omega
and
\Omega^{*}
and independence between
\phi^{D}_{\Omega}
and
\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}
we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

By remark
5.6
we have
\mathcal{A}^{P}_{\Omega}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)\mathbb{E}[\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)}|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}](\varphi)
where
\mathbb{E}
is taken under
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. Indeed, one could argue alternatively since

\frac{\mathrm{d}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\big(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}\big)}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big[\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}\big],

(6.19)

and by lemma
5.15
the map
\tau_{\Sigma}
generates
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}
whence
\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}|\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma})=\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega}
. This viewpoint however plays no essential role in the proofs.

Trace Axiom and its Consequences

In this subsection we treat separately (ii) of lemma
6.1
as part of (iii) of definition
6.1
. Let
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
,
not necessarily connected
. We will consider two closed surfaces:
\check{\Omega}
and
|\Omega|^{2}:=(\Omega^{*}\cup_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}\Omega)^{\vee}
.

Proposition 6.3

(pre-trace)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=\frac{\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.20)

Proof.

This boils down to comparing the explicit expressions for the densities as given by corollary
2.15
. Indeed, the relation that we need is

\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi},(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma)}=2\big\langle\varphi,(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

(6.21)

This is true for
\varphi\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
because
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}=\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}\oplus\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
and
\langle[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}],\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}]\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma)}
and
\langle\varphi,\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
are both the Dirichlet energy of the harmonic extension over
\Omega
with boundary condition
(\varphi,\varphi)
. The equality then extends to
\varphi\in W^{-\delta}(\Sigma)
for small enough
\delta
by continuity (see lemma
2.6
).
∎

Corollary 6.4

(trace for free field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.22)

Proof.

Indeed,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}\oplus 2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\int\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\underbrace{\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)}_{=\penalty\ 1}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

(BFK for
|\Omega|^{2}
)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS},

(BFK for
\check{\Omega}
)

finishing the proof.
∎

Note that we do not insist that
\Omega
be connected. This leads to the following important consequence of proposition
6.3
.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
be two dualizable surfaces such that
\partial\Omega_{1}=\partial\Omega_{2}=\Sigma
, all with the same co-orientation. Denote
M:=\Omega_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\Omega_{2}
, namely
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
glued along
\Sigma
. Then

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=\frac{\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{1}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{2}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.23)

Proof.

Indeed, in this case
\Omega:=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
can be seen as an element of
\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
. Then
|\Omega|^{2}=|\Omega_{1}|^{2}\sqcup|\Omega_{2}|^{2}
and the GFFs on the two components are independent. In addition,
\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
is the direct sum
\DN_{\Omega_{1}}^{\Sigma}\oplus\DN_{\Omega_{2}}^{\Sigma}
. Thus (
6.20
) gives (
6.23
).
∎

Corollary 6.6

(dissection gluing)
.

In the same situation as corollary
6.5
, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.24)

Proof.

Again, we apply corollary
6.4
directly to the case
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
and
|\Omega|^{2}=|\Omega_{1}|^{2}\sqcup|\Omega_{2}|^{2}
. We note that
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{0}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)
because

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)

(6.25)

and
\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}+m^{2})\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}+m^{2})
.
∎

Next we deal with the trace axiom in the interacting case. Again let
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
and consider
\check{\Omega}
. One has in this case the decomposition

\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})

(6.26)

and against which

S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi)=S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\PI_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)=S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\textrm{e}^{-S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\PI_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)}=\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}

(6.27)

for
\phi^{D}_{\Omega}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
and
\varphi\sim\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})
almost surely (see also remark
4.6
and
5.11
).

Corollary 6.7

(trace for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=Z_{\check{\Omega}}.

(6.28)

Proof.

Indeed,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(6.29)

therefore, with the constants involving determinants working out in exactly the same way as corollary
6.4
, one has

LHS

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})(\phi^{D}_{\Omega},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}}[\textrm{e}^{-S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi)}]=\textrm{RHS}.

We arrive at the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.8

(dissection gluing for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the same situation as corollary
6.5
, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=Z_{M}.

(6.30)

Proof.

One verifies that for
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{P}(\varphi).

(6.31)

This in fact also comes directly from the definition as

S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=S_{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})+S_{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})

(6.32)

pointwise almost surely with
\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}=\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}+\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}
. One then follows a verbatim reasoning as for corollary
6.6
.
∎

Bayes-Type Density Formulae and Gluing with Free Ends

In fact let us first put ourselves in the general situation of (iii) of definition
6.1
. Suppose the Riemannian surface
\Omega
is such that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}\sqcup\Sigma_{1}
and that
\rho:\Sigma_{2}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{2}^{*}
is an orientation reversing isometry. Denote by
\Omega/\rho
the surface obtained from
\Omega
by gluing
\Sigma_{2}
with
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
along
\rho
. Denote by
\Sigma_{4}
the reflected copy of
\Sigma_{2}
in
|\Omega/\rho|^{2}
.

Notation
.

In general, for
M
a closed manifold and
\Sigma_{1}
, …,
\Sigma_{\ell}\subset M
finitely many nonintersecting closed embedded hypersurfaces, we denote

\displaystyle\tau_{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{the trace map }C^{\infty}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{i})\times C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots,\textrm{ and its extensions,}

\displaystyle\PI_{M}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{the Poisson integral operator }W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i})\times W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots\longrightarrow W^{1}(M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{M,k\sqcup\cdots}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{k\sqcup\cdots}\PI_{M}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots},\textrm{ the transition operator }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{i})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{k})\times\cdots\textrm{ as in (\ref{eqn-trans-op-def}).}

We also remind the reader of the notations set up in definitions
4.7
and
4.4
.

Proposition 6.9

(Bayes with free ends)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(x)\bigg)^{2}=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}}(x,x,\varphi_{1})

(6.33)

Figure 2:
two ways of taking trace

Proof.

Indeed, by reflection symmetry across
\Sigma_{1}
where
\Sigma_{2}
gets identified with
\Sigma_{4}
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\varphi_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},4}^{1}\varphi_{1},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}=\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{4},\Omega/\rho,D},

(6.34)

under the symmetry. Also by symmetry (method of images)
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 4}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{x}{ x}=\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{x}{ x},

(6.35)

because both of them expresses the associated Dirichlet data on
\Sigma_{1}
of the solution of the Helmholtz boundary value problem over
\Omega
with Dirichlet data equal to
x
on both
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
and Neumann condition (zero normal derivative) on
\Sigma_{1}
. Thus,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 4\sqcup 1}}(x,x,\varphi_{1})

(formula (
5.25
) backward)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}}}(x,x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 4}[\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},\Omega,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})

(formula (
5.26
) forward)

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}(x,x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}}[\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},\Omega,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})

(#)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS}.

We see that with the help of Bayes principle we “glued away” the free end and we are reduced to the situation of proposition
6.3
. Indeed, at step (#) we use proposition
6.3
twice with respect to
|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}
which is
\Omega
“reflected twice” by first gluing
\Omega
with a reflected copy along
\Sigma_{1}
to get
\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}
, and then reflect and glue again along
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}^{*}
, with
\Sigma_{4}
,
\Sigma_{4}^{*}
being the reflected copies of
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
across
\Sigma_{1}
. From this we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}}}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}}}(x,x,x,x)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}(x,x),

finishing the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.10

(free-end gluing for free field)
.

We have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi),

(6.36)

for almost every
\psi\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}
.

Proof.

The key point is to disintegrate
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)
into a part involving only
\varphi
, which one could “integrate out” cleanly, multiplied by a part independent of
\varphi
, using proposition
6.9
. Indeed,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\int\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\underbrace{\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)}_{=\penalty\ 1}

\displaystyle=\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}\setminus\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*},D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(BFK for
|\Omega|^{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}
)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS},

(BFK for
|\Omega/\rho|^{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}
)

finishing the proof.
∎

Before proceeding to the interacting case we remind the reader of remark
5.11
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For fixed
\psi\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{1})
, then the random fields

\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{1}\psi+\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{2,D}\tau_{2}\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{\phi}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\PI_{\Omega}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}[\varphi,\varphi,\psi]

(6.37)

with
\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D}
and
\varphi\sim(\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}
, follow the same law on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
.

Proof.

Here
\Omega^{\circ}
is also
(\Omega/\rho)\setminus\Sigma_{2}
. Indeed, both
\phi
and
\widetilde{\phi}
solves the stochastic boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})u=0&\textrm{in }\Omega^{\circ},\\
u|_{\Sigma_{2}}=u|_{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}\sim(\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D},&\textrm{on }\Sigma_{2}\textrm{ or }\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}\\
u|_{\Sigma_{1}}=\psi,&\textrm{on }\Sigma_{1},\end{array}\right.

(6.38)

with equalities holding almost surely.
∎

Corollary 6.12

(free-end gluing for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{P}(\psi),

(6.39)

for almost every
\psi\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}
.

Proof.

Indeed,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi,\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(6.40)

therefore, with the constants involving determinants working out in exactly the same way as corollary
6.10
, one has

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi,\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega/\rho}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}}|\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mathrm{d}\big[(\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{2,D}\tau_{2})_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D})\big](\phi^{D}_{\Omega},(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}})

(lemma
6.11
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega/\rho}(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D}|\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D}(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})=\textrm{RHS},

where the notation
(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}}
is as in proposition
5.8
. We arrive at the proof.
∎

Now let
\Omega_{1}\in\Mor(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
,
\Omega_{2}\in\Mor(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})
and
|\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1}|^{2}:=(\Omega_{1}^{*}\cup_{4}\Omega_{2}^{*}\cup_{3}\Omega_{2}\cup_{2}\Omega_{1})^{\vee}
, where we denote by
\Sigma_{4}
the “glued” outgoing and incoming boundaries of
\Omega_{2}^{*}
and
\Omega_{1}^{*}
. In this case the result could be seen as a special case of the previous one, where
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
has two disjoint components. We shall re-state these results without proofs.

Corollary 6.13
.

We have

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1\sqcup 3}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{3})\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1}|^{2},2}^{1\sqcup 3}[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{3}\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1},D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1}|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{1}}^{\Sigma_{2},N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{2}}^{\Sigma_{2},N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}}(x,\varphi_{3}).\quad\Box

Corollary 6.14

(composition for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\psi_{1},\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{P}(\varphi,\psi_{3})\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}\cup_{2}\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\psi_{1},\psi_{3}),

(6.41)

for almost every
(\psi_{1},\psi_{3})\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}
.
\Box

Periodic Cover = Spin Chain

Geometric setting

Our set-up corresponds to example 1 in Bergeron
[
9
]
. Let
M
be a closed oriented Riemannian surface
6
6

6

In particular its
\mathbb{Z}
-homology groups are non-torsion.
of genus
g\geqslant 1
and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded
primitive
closed geodesic whose
\mathbb{Z}
-homology class is non-trivial (exists by a classical theorem of E. Cartan). Necessarily,
\Sigma
is nondissecting.
7
7

7

If
\Sigma
dissects
M
into
M_{+}^{\circ}\sqcup M_{-}^{\circ}
, then
\Sigma=\partial M_{+}
; now closed 1-forms integrate to zero over
\Sigma
by Stokes theorem, thus
\Sigma
is null-homologous via de Rham’s theorem. Alternatively, note
\partial:H_{2}(M_{+},\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
in the exact sequence of the pair
(M_{+},\Sigma)
is surjective, producing the fundamental class.
We consider the covering space
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow M
over
M
given by a (normal) subgroup
\ker\rho
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M)
where
\rho
is the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho:\pi_{1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ab}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(-{,}{[}\Sigma{]})}

(7.1)

where the first is Abelianization and
I(-,[\Sigma])
is the
oriented intersection number
8
8

8

I([\gamma],[\Sigma])=D(D^{-1}[\gamma]\smile D^{-1}[\Sigma])=\int_{\gamma}\eta_{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
D^{-1}
is the Poincaré dual map and
\eta_{\Sigma}
is a smooth bump 1-form supported in a tubular neighborhood of
\Sigma
such that
\int_{\Sigma}\alpha=\int_{M}\alpha\wedge\eta_{\Sigma}
for any 1-form
\alpha
.
with
\Sigma
, which is surjective (since
\Sigma
is primitive). In other words, we put
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}=\ker\rho\setminus\widetilde{M}
where
\widetilde{M}
is the universal cover of
M
and
\ker\rho
acts on
\widetilde{M}
as deck transformations. Equip
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
with the covering metric (thus deck transformations act by isometries).

Geometrically,
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
can be understood as first cutting
M
along
\Sigma
and obtaining the surface
\Omega:=M\setminus\Sigma
with boundaries
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cong\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\cong\Sigma
, and gluing
\Omega
periodically where each
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is glued to the “next”
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
. Indeed, the class
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(M)
of a loop
\gamma
is in
\ker\rho
iff
I([\gamma],[\Sigma])=0
; in other words, these loops are exactly those which are “not cut”, i.e. lifts to a loop on
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
, and loops which do intersect
\Sigma
are lifted to segments whose end points are related by a deck transformation, i.e. they are “cut”.

Now, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, compose
\rho
further with the mod
N
map
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}=:\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
and denote it by
\rho_{N}
, and let the covering space of
M
corresponding to
\ker\rho_{N}
be
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
. Since
\ker\rho\subset\ker\rho_{N}
,
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
also covers
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
. Geometrically, this corresponds to closing the surface after gluing
N
copies of
\Omega
— loops that intersect
\Sigma

N
-times are now lifted to a “big loop” in
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
.

Figure 3:
periodic covering and cyclic covering

Remark 7.1
.

We also say that the sequence of covers
(M_{N}^{\Sigma})_{N}

converges
to
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
.

That
\Omega:=M\setminus\Sigma
should be understood for what follows.

Continued Introduction of Spin Chain Example

Here we continue our discussion of the circular spin chain proposed in section
1.1
, in particular the equation (
1.6
). More generally, one can insert “nice” functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}
in between at the sites
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leqslant N
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F_{k}(\sigma(i_{k}))\cdots F_{1}(\sigma(i_{1}))\textrm{e}^{-S(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma=\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(T^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}T^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}T^{i_{1}-1}\right),

(7.2)

where
F_{j}
are thought of as multiplication operators. The evaluation

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}F_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(N)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F_{k}(\sigma(i_{k}))\cdots F_{1}(\sigma(i_{1}))\textrm{e}^{-S(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(T^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}T^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}T^{i_{1}-1}\right)}{\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(T^{N})}\end{array}\right.

(7.3)

is said to define a
Gibbs state
of our spin chain on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. Alternatively this is the expected value functional under the discrete Gibbs measure (
1.2
).

Definition 7.1
.

We say that a Gibbs state exists in the
thermodynamic limit
if the second expression in (
7.3
) tends to a limit as
N\to\infty
for bounded functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

We can see from (
1.4
) that the operator
T
is not just bounded, smoothing, but its kernel is strictly positive. Such an operator has a special property which we call the
Perron-Frobenius
property, referring to the consequence of proposition
7.1
below. In particular, this would ensure that the thermodynamic limit does exist, as we shall see in corollaries
7.3
,
7.4
, and
7.5
.

Remark 7.2
.

Corollary
7.3
could be equivalently understood as saying that for compactly supported observables
(F,G)
, and
(\tau\sigma)(i):=\sigma(i+1)
the shift operator, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\tau^{k}\sigma)G(\sigma)]=\mathbb{E}[F]\mathbb{E}[G]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{k})

with
\alpha<1
the same as in corollary
7.3
. Here the expected value should be thought of as coming from a Gibbs measure on the infinite path space
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}
over
\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, by definition
7.1
, this is exactly the vague limit of the finite dimensional (periodic) Gibbs measures over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. We say that with respect to this Gibbs measure the shift operator is
exponentially mixing
.

With transfer operator being Perron-Frobenius, the partition functions as in (
1.2
) also enjoy explicit asymptotics. We will explore a consequence in the case of periodic surfaces in the last section.

Example 7.1
.

In the case
P(\sigma)=m^{2}\sigma^{2}
, the spin chain is the discrete massive GFF. We have an exact formula for the partition function
\mathcal{Z}(N)=\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}(1+m^{2}-\cos(2\pi\frac{k}{N}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

hence

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\mathcal{Z}(N)\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\log(1+m^{2}-\cos(2\pi x))\mathrm{d}x.

Perron-Frobenius Property and Gibbs State

Remark 7.3
.

A large part of this section could as well be included in the functional analysis appendix under the title “properties of a Perron-Frobenius operator”. But we include them here as they form an integral part of the discussion of physical phenomena.

We remind the reader of remark
1.3
.

Definition 7.2
.

An operator
A
on
L^{2}(Q,\mu)
of some measure space
(Q,\mu)
has
strictly positive kernel
if for any nonnegative
F\in L^{2}(Q,\mu)
such that
\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\neq 0
we have
AF>0
almost surely.

Proposition 7.1

(Perron-Frobenius,
[
27
]
page 51)
.

If
A
on
L^{2}(Q,\mu)
has strictly positive kernel, and
\lambda=\left\lVert A\right\rVert
is an eigenvalue of
A
, then
\lambda
is simple, and the corresponding eigenvector can be chosen to be strictly positive almost surely.
\Box

By our definition of the amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}
as the square-root of the Radon-Nikodym density between two mutually absolutely continuous positive finite measures (
\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}}
is almost surely positive since
S_{\Omega}
is a real-valued random variable, recall also remark
2.7
that the zeta-determinants are positive), we get immediately

Lemma 7.2
.

For any traceable cobordism
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
, the Segal transfer operator
U_{\Omega}:L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
has strictly positive kernel.
\Box

We deduce that
U_{\Omega}
has a simple top eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=\left\lVert U_{\Omega}\right\rVert
spanned by a normalized, almost surely strictly positive eigenvector
\Omega_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
. Alternatively speaking
U_{\Omega}
has a
spectral gap
. We get

Corollary 7.3
.

Denote
\widehat{U}_{\Omega}:=\lambda_{0}^{-1}U_{\Omega}
, let
\lambda_{1}
be the eigenvalue of
U_{\Omega}
with next largest modulus, thus
\lambda_{0}>|\lambda_{1}|
, and put
\alpha:=|\lambda_{1}|/\lambda_{0}<1
. Then for any
F
,
G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
, we have

\big|\big\langle F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}G\big\rangle-\big\langle F,\Omega_{0}\big\rangle\big\langle\Omega_{0},G\big\rangle\big|\leqslant\alpha^{N}\left\lVert F\right\rVert\left\lVert G\right\rVert.

(7.4)

Proof.

Note
\langle F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}G\rangle-\left\langle F,\Omega_{0}\right\rangle\left\langle\Omega_{0},G\right\rangle=\langle\Pi_{0}^{\perp}F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}\Pi_{0}^{\perp}G\rangle
where
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection onto the complement of
\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}
, where
\widehat{U}_{\Omega}
has norm
\alpha<1
.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

We have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})=\log\lambda_{0}.

(7.5)

Proof.

Without loss of generality let
N\geqslant 2
so each
U_{\Omega}^{N}
is trace class. On one hand

\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})\leqslant\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{N-2}\big\rVert\leqslant\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\lambda_{0}^{N-2}.

(7.6)

On the other, we decompose
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}=\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}\oplus\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}
where

\displaystyle\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}^{N}+\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\lambda_{0}^{N}-\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}|\lambda_{1}|^{N-2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}^{N}\left(1-C_{1}\alpha^{N-2}\lambda_{0}^{-2}\right).

This and (
7.6
) gives the result after taking
N\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary
7.3
and the proof of corollary
7.4
implies

Corollary 7.5
.

For any bounded operator
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma})
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N-L}F)}{\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{L}}\left\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\right\rangle=\frac{\left\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\Omega_{0},U_{\Omega}^{L}\Omega_{0}\right\rangle}.

(7.7)

Proof.

Remember that
N\gg L
. Thus by
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}=\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}\oplus\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}
and (iv) of lemma
A.3
we have

\lambda_{0}^{-N+L}\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\big)=\big\langle\Omega_{0},\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\Omega_{0}\big\rangle+\ttr\big(\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big)

(7.8)

with

\big|\ttr\big(\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big)\big|\leqslant\left\lVert F\right\rVert\big\lVert\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big\rVert\to 0.

(7.9)

Since
\langle\Omega_{0},\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\Omega_{0}\rangle\to\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\rangle
by corollary
7.3
and
\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})\asymp\lambda_{0}^{N}
by corollary
7.4
, we obtain the result.
∎

We arrive at the conclusion that, for functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
and integers
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
, the evaluation

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}F_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}U_{\Omega}^{i_{1}-1}\big)}{\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N}\big)}\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\big\langle\Omega_{0},F_{k}U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}U_{\Omega}^{i_{1}-1}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle}{\big\langle\Omega_{0},U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-1}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle}\end{array}\right.

(7.10)

defines a Gibbs state in the thermodynamic limit on a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
-valued
\mathbb{Z}
-spin chain.

Remark 7.4
.

In fact, a valid functional
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
could be given by

F(\psi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\psi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\int\widetilde{F}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega},\psi,\varphi)\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\psi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(7.11)

for
\widetilde{F}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ}),\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})
, with
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
considered as the induced measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
via
\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
. Hence the Gibbs state actually extends to the continuum
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
.

Asymptotic of Partition Function

For the technical reason of remark
A.2
we shall assume the surface
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
be
reflection symmetric
, which in simplest words means
\Omega=\widetilde{\Omega}^{*}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Omega}
for some
\widetilde{\Omega}\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})
, in the notations of lemma
6.1
. In other words there is an isometric involution
\Theta:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
whose fixed point set is exactly
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and exchanges the two components of
\partial\Omega
. This is not much of a restriction. In this case
U_{\Omega}=U_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{*}}\circ U_{\widetilde{\Omega}}
and lemma
A.7
applies, namely
\ttr(U_{\Omega})=\ttr_{\rho}(U_{\Omega})
, as well as for
U_{\Omega}^{N}
. From corollary
6.7
and corollary
7.4
we then deduce that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log(Z_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}})=\log\lambda_{0}.

(7.12)

This applies in particular to the free case where
Z_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}}=\detz(\Delta_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Remark 7.5
.

There arises the interesting question of how
\lambda_{0}
(or
\log\lambda_{0}
) would depend on the geometry of
\Omega
. Very crudely one would expect
\log\lambda_{0}\propto\vol(\Omega)
, since in our case the corresponding
\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}
for
N\Omega
(
N
copies of
\Omega
glued) is just
\lambda_{0}^{N}
. A more precise formula in the general, non-periodic case seems desirable.

Appendix A
Functional Analysis

Tensor Product, Fock Space, Ideals

While leaving details to Simon
[
71
]
, Reed and Simon
[
58
]
, and Janson
[
40
]
, we make the following conventions and definitions. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space,
v_{i}
,
w_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. Hilbert spaces are assumed real in the sequel.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{symmetric group on }n\textrm{ letters};

(A.1)

\displaystyle v_{1}\odot\dots\odot v_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sym^{n}(v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{n})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}v_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{\sigma(n)},

(A.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle v_{1}\odot\dots\odot v_{n},w_{1}\odot\dots\odot w_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\odot n}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle v_{i},w_{\sigma(i)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(A.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\odot n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\overline{\sym^{n}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n})}\textrm{ under (\ref{eqn-sym-hilb-inner-prod})}

(A.4)

\displaystyle\alpha\odot\beta\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sym^{n+m}(\alpha\otimes\beta),\textrm{ for }\alpha\in\mathcal{H}^{\odot n},\beta\in\mathcal{H}^{\odot m}.

(A.5)

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space. Then the
Boson Fock space
\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
associated to
\mathcal{H}
is defined to be the Hilbert space direct sum

\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}^{\odot n},

(A.6)

that is,

\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\{(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots)\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{\odot i},\penalty\ \sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\alpha_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\odot i}}^{2}<\infty\right\},

(A.7)

where, as usual, we put
\mathcal{H}^{\odot 0}=\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
, and the unit length generator of this bottom space is called the
vacuum state
, denoted
|0\rangle
or
\Omega_{0}
.

Proposition A.1

(
[
40
]
theorem 4.5,
[
72
]
corollary I.15)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces and
A:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
a bounded operator. Then, for each
n\geqslant 0
, there exists a unique bounded operator
\Gamma_{n}(A):\mathcal{H}^{\odot n}\to\mathcal{K}^{\odot n}
such that

\Gamma_{n}(A)(v_{1}\odot\dots\odot v_{n})=Av_{1}\odot\dots\odot Av_{n},

(A.8)

for all
v_{1}
, …,
v_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
, and one has
\left\lVert\Gamma_{n}(A)\right\rVert=\left\lVert A\right\rVert^{n}
. Moreover, if
A
is a
contraction
, namely if
\left\lVert A\right\rVert\leqslant 1
, then the direct sum

\Gamma(A)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\Gamma_{n}(A):\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{K}),

(A.9)

that is,
\Gamma(A)(\dots,\alpha_{k},\dots):=(\dots,\Gamma_{k}(A)\alpha_{k},\dots)
, is a bounded operator with norm
1
.

Proposition A.2

(
*
-homomorphism,
[
40
]
page 45)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{L}
be Hilbert spaces and
A:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
,
B:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{L}
operators with
\left\lVert A\right\rVert
,
\left\lVert B\right\rVert\leqslant 1
. Then

1.

\Gamma(BA)=\Gamma(B)\Gamma(A)
as operators
\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
;

2.

\Gamma(A^{*})=\Gamma(A)^{*}:\Gamma(\mathcal{K})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
,
(\bullet)^{*}
being the adjoint;

3.

\Gamma(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\Gamma(\mathcal{H})}
for any
\mathcal{H}
.

Remark A.1
.

The map
\Gamma
should not be confused with the
second quantization

\mathrm{d}\Gamma
explained on Simon
[
72
]
page 31.

Let
A:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}
be a compact operator and
A^{*}:\mathcal{K}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
its adjoint. Then
A^{*}A
is a compact self-adjoint nonnegative operator whose spectrum consists of positive eigenvalues
\mu_{0}(A)^{2}\geqslant\mu_{1}(A)^{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant 0
with the only possible accumulation point being zero, which may or may not be in the spectrum or an eigenvalue. The numbers
\mu_{n}(A)
are called
singular values
of
A
. Define

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Big(\sum_{n}\mu_{n}(A)^{p}\Big)^{1/p},

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{for }1\leqslant p<\infty,

(A.10)

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\lVert A\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{for }p=\infty.

(A.11)

Put
\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}):=\{A\textrm{ compact}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}<\infty\}
. We shall be concerned only with
\mathcal{J}_{1}
, where the norm is also denoted
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
, called the
trace class
operators and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
, called the
Hilbert-Schmidt
operators, where the norm is also written
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
. When
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{H}
, define also

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n}\left\langle e_{n},Ae_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(A.12)

where
\{e_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. Its convergence and (in)dependence on basis is discussed below. When
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{K}
we use the notation
\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
.

Lemma A.3

(
[
71
]
theorems 2.7, 2.8, 2.14, 3.1,
[
27
]
page 132-133)
.

We have

1.

whenever
A:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is bounded and self-adjoint nonnegative, the sum (
A.12
) valuing in
[0,+\infty]
is independent of bases; it is finite iff
A\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
in which case
\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
;

2.

A\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
iff
(A^{*}A)^{p/2}\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, in which case
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}=\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}((A^{*}A)^{p/2})^{1/p}
. In particular,
A
is Hilbert-Schmidt iff
A^{*}A
is trace class.

3.

Whenever
A
,
C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
B\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
we have
\left\lVert ABC\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert\left\lVert C\right\rVert\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}
; thus each
\mathcal{J}_{p}
is a two-sided ideal in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
;

4.

if
A\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
then the sum (
A.12
) converges absolutely, is independent of bases, and
|\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)|\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
;

5.

whenever
p^{-1}=q^{-1}+r^{-1}
and
A\in\mathcal{J}_{q}(\mathcal{H})
,
B\in\mathcal{J}_{r}(\mathcal{H})
then
\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{q}}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{r}}
and
AB\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
; in particular, the product of two Hilbert-Schmidt operators is trace class.

Hilbert-Schmidt Operators on
L^{2}

Proposition A.4

(
[
58
]
page 52)
.

Let
(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be measure spaces so that
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
are separable. Then the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\times L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})&\longrightarrow&L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}),\\
(f,g)&\longmapsto&fg,\end{array}\right.

(A.13)

with
(fg)(x,y):=f(x)g(y)
extends to a unique isomorphism
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\otimes L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})\cong L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})
.

Proposition A.5

(
[
58
]
page 220,
[
73
]
theorem 3.8.4)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces. Then the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{K}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}),\\
(v,w)&\longmapsto&\left\langle v,-\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}w\end{array}\right.

(A.14)

extends to a unique isometric isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}\cong\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. In particular, for every
\eta\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}
there exist orthonormal sets
\{\varphi_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{H}
,
\{\psi_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{K}
and real numbers
\lambda_{n}>0
so that

\eta=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}(\varphi_{n}\otimes\psi_{n}),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{I}(\eta)(v)=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}\left\langle\varphi_{n},v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\psi_{n},

(A.15)

and vice versa.

Corollary A.6

(
[
73
]
theorem 3.8.5)
.

Let
(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be measure spaces so that
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
are separable. Then there is an isometric isomorphism

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{I}:L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{J}_{2}(L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1}),L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})),\\
K(x,y)&\longmapsto&\Big[f\longmapsto\displaystyle\int K(x,-)f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\Big].\end{array}\right.

(A.16)

In particular, there exist orthonormal families
\{\varphi_{n}\}\subset L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
\{\psi_{n}\}\subset L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
so that

K(x,y)=\sum_{n}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{I}(K))\overline{\varphi_{n}(x)}\psi_{n}(y),

(A.17)

with the latter series converging absolutely in
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
for almost every fixed
y
, and
\mu_{n}(\mathcal{I}(K))
denote the singular values of
\mathcal{I}(K)
.

Now (ii) of lemma
A.3
implies that

Corollary A.7
.

Let
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be as above and
A:L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\longrightarrow L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, represented via (
A.16
) by the integral kernel
K_{A}(x,y)
. Then

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\ttr_{L^{2}(Q_{1})}(A^{*}A)=\int|K_{A}(x,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(y).\quad\Box

(A.18)

Remark A.2
.

For general
A\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(Q))
or
\mathcal{J}_{2}(L^{2}(Q))
, the fact that
\int|K_{A}(x,x)|\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
does not imply
A
is trace class, and nor does the trace equal
\int K_{A}(x,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
when
A
is trace class (since the diagonal has measure zero in
Q\times Q
, in reality one could let
K_{A}(x,x)
be arbitrary without affecting
A
, thus an important condition is that
K_{A}
be continuous in “some sense”). See Simon
[
73
]
section 3.11, also Vershik, Petrov and Zatitskiy
[
54
]
section 3.3.

Appendix B
Gaussian Analysis

Basic Definitions and Spaces

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet space and
\mathcal{X}^{*}
the dual space of continuous linear functionals. Equip
\mathcal{X}
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Definition B.1
.

A probability measure
\gamma
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
is called (centered)
Gaussian
if every linear functional
f\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
,
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, is a (centered) Gaussian random variable on
\mathcal{X}
.

Random variables and measures are assumed centered in the sequel unless otherwise stated.

Definition B.2
.

A
(general) Gaussian Hilbert space
is any closed linear subspace of
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
of any probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
, consisting of (centered) Gaussian variables.

Definition B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet (or Banach) space, and
\gamma
a Gaussian measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Then the closure of
\mathcal{X}^{*}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma)
is called the
Gaussian Hilbert space of
\gamma
, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
.

An important gadget for identifying Gaussian measures is the (inverse) Fourier transform, more frequently called the
charateristic function
,
\widehat{\gamma}:\mathcal{X}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, defined as

\widehat{\gamma}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi(f)}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\phi).

(B.1)

This is useful because of the following property.

Lemma B.1

(
[
13
]
theorem 2.2.4, proposition A.3.18)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet (or Banach) space, then any two measures on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
with equal characteristic functions coincide. In particular, a probability measure
\gamma
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a centered Gaussian measure if and only if

\widehat{\gamma}(f)=\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)^{2}]}

(B.2)

for all
f\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, where
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}
is the expectation with respect to
\gamma
.

Remark B.1
.

The variance
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)^{2}]
of
\phi(f)
appears in (
B.2
). Thus lemma
B.1
implies that two (centered) Gaussian measures on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
with the same
covariance structure
must coincide; that is, if

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]

(B.3)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, then
\gamma=\widetilde{\gamma}
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be equipped with a Gaussian measure
\gamma
. Then in
\mathcal{X}
sits an important subspace
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
, which is a Hilbert space with norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}
, called the
Cameron-Martin space
, that does the following job. For two measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we write “
\mu\approx\nu
” to mean
\mu
and
\nu
are
mutually absolutely continuous
, and “
\mu\perp\nu
” to mean
\mu
and
\nu
are mutually singular.

Proposition B.2

(Cameron-Martin,
[
13
]
corollary 2.4.3)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet space,
\gamma
a Gaussian measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
, and
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})\subset\mathcal{X}
the Cameron-Martin space. For any
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
, define the measure
(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
by setting

(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma(B)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\gamma(B-\phi_{0}),

(B.4)

where
B-\phi_{0}=\{\phi-\phi_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \phi\in B\}
. Then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma\approx\gamma,&\textrm{if }\phi_{0}\in C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}),\\
(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma\perp\gamma,&\textrm{if }\phi_{0}\not\in C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}).\end{array}\right.

(B.5)

Moreover, in the first case, one has the Radon-Nikodym density

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}(\phi)=\textrm{e}^{(C^{-1}\phi_{0})(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\phi_{0}\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}^{2}},

(B.6)

where
C
is the operator defined in (
B.7
).

Now, how to locate the space
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
in
\mathcal{X}
? The answer is that we can first locate it in
\mathcal{X}^{**}
(the double dual), where each element of
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
turns out to be
weak* continuous
(Bogachev
[
13
]
page 362), and since
\mathcal{X}
is locally convex, it is actually in
\mathcal{X}
. Starting from the Gaussian Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
we define the
covariance operator

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}C:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{X}^{**},\\
f&\longmapsto&\big(C(f):h\longmapsto\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]\big).\end{array}\right.

(B.7)

In other words
C(f)
is the linear functional
\left\langle f,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\gamma)}
on
\mathcal{X}^{*}
.

Lemma B.3

(
[
13
]
page 44, sections 2.4, 3.2)
.

1.

the map
C
is injective.

2.

Equip
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
with an inner product by requiring
C
to be isometry, namely

\big\langle f,h\big\rangle_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\big\langle C^{-1}f,C^{-1}h\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}.

(B.8)

Then
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
is a complete Hilbert space,
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
is continuous, and each
\phi\longmapsto\phi(f)
,
f\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, is a continuous linear functional for
\phi\in C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
.

3.

Equivalently, the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}
is

\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{X}^{*},f\neq 0}\frac{|\phi(f)|}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}}.

(B.9)

Thus
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
is precisely the dual of
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
under the evaluation pairing
(\phi,f)\longmapsto\phi(f)
.
\Box

Q
-spaces and Their Equivalence

A
Q
-space
is in other words a probability sample space. The GFF on
M
, say, can be seen (abstractly) as a way of associating a Gaussian random variable
\phi(f)
to each
f\in W^{-1}(M)
so that (
2.6
) holds. This says nothing about the sample space on which these random variables are actually defined, and naturally there exist many choices. For example,

1.

the Bochner-Minlos construction, mentioned as proposition
2.1
;

2.

the formal Fourier series construction, mentioned in remark
2.2
; this can be identified with the previous one by appealing to the condition under which a formal Fourier series represents an actual distribution, see Shubin
[
69
]
page 92 proposition 10.2;

3.

the
abstract Wiener space
construction. While this is not used essentially in this paper, it is a way of constructing (recovering) a separable Banach
Q
-space
\mathcal{X}
starting from (knowing) the Cameron-Martin space, and taking closure with respect to a carefully defined norm weaker than
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}
. See Sheffield
[
68
]
and Bogachev
[
13
]
section 3.9 for details.

Some other models are discussed in Simon
[
72
]
section I.2, to which we refer for details in general. We shall discuss the question of in what sense two models of
Q
-spaces are equivalent, though all the three examples above could eventually by realized in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
. This is useful concerning the decomposition (
5.11
), and we give the precise sense in which the original
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
could be recovered from the decomposed measure
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}
.

Definition B.4

(
[
72
]
page 4)
.

Two probability measure spaces
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are called
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism of
measure algebras

T:\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu^{\prime}},

(B.10)

here
\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mu^{\prime}}
being the ideals of measure zero sets of
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
, such that
\mu^{\prime}(T(A))=\mu(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
(we do not distinguish an event
A
from its class in
\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
).

Remark B.2
.

Mutually absolutely continuous measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{O}
will define the same measure algebra
\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
as they have the same measure zero sets.

Definition B.5

(
[
72
]
page 5)
.

If
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are isomorphic under
T
, then two random variables
f:Q\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
f^{\prime}:Q^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

correspond under the isomorphism
if

T(f^{-1}(B))=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(B)

(B.11)

for all Borel sets
B\subset\mathbb{R}
.

For a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a (centered)
Gaussian process indexed by

\mathcal{H}
is a family of centered Gaussian random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H}\}
so that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(B.12)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Such a process is
defined on
the probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
if each
\phi(f)
is a random variable from
Q
and
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H}\}
generates
\mathcal{O}
.

Proposition B.4

(
[
72
]
theorem I.6)
.

Let
\{\phi(f)\}
and
\{\phi^{\prime}(f)\}
be two Gaussian processes indexed by
\mathcal{H}
defined respectively on
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
. Then there is an isomorphism between the two probability spaces so that
\phi(f)
corresponds to
\phi^{\prime}(f)
under the isomorphism for each
f\in\mathcal{H}
.

Remark for proof.

Note for probability spaces
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)\supset L^{\infty}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)\supset\{\textrm{indicators}\}
. Similarly for
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
. Thus
\Gamma(U)
where
U
takes each
\phi(f)
to
\phi^{\prime}(f)
gives the isomorphism (corollary
B.10
).
∎

Proposition B.5

(
[
72
]
proposition I.7)
.

Let
\{\phi_{1}(f)\}
and
\{\phi_{2}(f)\}
be Gaussian processes, respectively, indexed by
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, defined on
(Q_{1},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mu_{2})
. Then a Gaussian process
\{\phi(h)\}
indexed by
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
can be defined on
Q:=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
equipped with
\mathcal{O}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}
, by putting

\phi(f_{1}\oplus f_{2})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{1}(f_{1})+\phi_{2}(f_{2}),

(B.13)

for all
f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Measurable Linear (Itô-Wiener) Extensions

Here we collect some results concerning the possibility of extending a linear operator defined on the Cameron-Martin space
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
to the whole
Q
-space
\mathcal{X}
, in a not necessarily “functional-analytic” sense. Such results, in a different guise, lie behind the possibility of defining the classical stochastic integrals à la Itô-Wiener.

Definition B.6

(
[
13
]
definition 3.7.1)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma)
be a Fréchet Gaussian
Q
-space and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
another Fréchet space equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Then a map
F:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is called a
\gamma
-measurable linear operator
if it agrees
\gamma
-almost surely with a
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
-measurable linear map
F_{0}:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
.

In particular,

Definition B.7

(
[
13
]
definition 2.10.1)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma)
be a Fréchet Gaussian
Q
-space. A
\gamma
-measurable linear functional
f
on
X
is called a
measurable linear functional
if there exist a full-measure linear subspace
\mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathcal{X}
such that
f
agrees
\gamma
-almost surely with a usual
\gamma
-measurable linear functional
f_{0}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Proposition B.6

(
[
13
]
theorem 2.10.11)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma)
be a real separable Fréchet Gaussian
Q
-space. Denote by
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
its Cameron-Martin space. Then every continuous linear functional
f
on
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
extends
uniquely
(modulo measure zero sets) to a measurable linear functional
\widehat{f}
on
\mathcal{X}
that coincides with
f
on
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
. Moreover,
\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}=\lVert f\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})^{*}}
.

Proposition B.7

(
[
13
]
theorem 3.7.6)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma)
be a real separable Fréchet Gaussian
Q
-space. Denote by
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})
its Cameron-Martin space. Then every operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}))
extends to a
\gamma
-measurable linear operator
\widehat{A}:(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{\gamma})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
, such that the measure image of
\gamma
under
\widehat{A}
is a Gaussian measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Here
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{\gamma}
denotes the Lebesgue completion of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
with respect to
\gamma
. Moreover, any two extensions of
A
which are
(\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{\gamma},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
-measurable and linear on full measure subspaces agree
\gamma
-almost surely.

The extension
\widehat{A}
obtained above will be called the
measurable linear (or Itô-Wiener) extension
of
A
.

Wiener Chaos and Wick’s Theorem

Proposition B.8

(Wiener Chaos decomposition,
[
40
]
theorem 2.6)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
a Gaussian Hilbert space. Then there is an orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})\cong\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}},

(B.14)

where
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by variables in
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(\mathcal{H})\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n-1}(\mathcal{H})^{\perp}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathcal{H})
denotes the span of polynomials of random variables in
\mathcal{H}
of degree
\leqslant j
; in particular
\mathcal{H}^{{:}0{:}}
denotes the constants.

If
F\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(\mathcal{H})
, denote by
{:}F{:}
the projection of
F
onto
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
, and is called a
Wick ordered polynomial
; for
F\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})
, denote by
I_{n}(F)
its projection onto
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
. Define the
Hermite polynomials

h_{n}(x)
by

\exp\Big(zx-\frac{1}{2}z^{2}\Big)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}h_{n}(x).

(B.15)

We have
h_{0}(x)=1
,
h_{2}(x)=x^{2}-1
,
h_{4}(x)=x^{4}-6x^{2}+3
, etc.

Lemma B.9

(Wick’s theorem/Feynman rules,
[
72
]
propositions I.2, I.3, I.4,
[
40
]
theorems 1.28, 3.9, 3.19)
.

1.

For
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
(not necessarily distinct) jointly Gaussian random variables,

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdots X_{n}]=\sum_{\mathscr{P}}\prod_{k}\mathbb{E}[X_{i_{k}}X_{j_{k}}],

(B.16)

where the sum is over all partitions
\mathscr{P}
of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
into disjoint pairs
\{i_{k},j_{k}\}
,
1\leqslant k\leqslant n/2
. In particular, the result is zero if
n
is odd.

2.

For
X\in\mathcal{H}
,

{:}X^{n}{:}=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{j}n!}{(n-2j)!j!2^{j}}\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{j}X^{n-2j}=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{n}{2}}h_{n}\big(X\big/\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(B.17)

3.

Let
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
and
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be jointly Gaussian random variables. Then

\mathbb{E}\big[{:}X_{1}\dots X_{n}{:}\penalty\ {:}Y_{1}\dots Y_{m}{:}\big]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Y_{\sigma(i)}],&&m=n,\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0,&&m\neq n.\end{aligned}\right.

(B.18)

In particular,
\mathbb{E}[{:}X^{n}{:}\penalty\ {:}Y^{m}{:}]=\delta_{nm}n!\mathbb{E}[XY]^{n}
.

There is a general way of associating random variables (or numbers) to
Feynman diagrams
. A
Feynman diagram
consists of vertices, legs (segments with only one end attached to a vertex), and edges (contracted legs). Two legs are
contracted
means they are connected to form an edge. A Feynman diagram is called
fully contracted
if there is no unconnected legs. Given
n
random variables
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}
, a Feynman diagram
labelled by

(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is simply any Feynman diagram whose vertices are in bijection with
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
. A Feynman diagram labelled by
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
can now be associated with
either
a random variable
or
a number using the following rules:

1.

for each leg attached to a vertex
j
, write down the corresponding random variable
X_{j}
, and multiply them all together;

2.

whenever two legs are contracted, enclose the corresponding two random variables by
\mathbb{E}[\bullet]
.

Thus fully contracted diagrams are always associated with numbers. If
\gamma
is a Feynman diagram labelled by
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}
, denote by
v(\gamma)
the associated object following the above rules.

Example B.1

(
[
52
]
section 4.4)
.

In a physical context Feynman diagrams are used in rather formal calculations. We would like to find

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\int_{M}\phi(x)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}\big]

(B.19)

by Taylor expanding
\exp(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\int_{M}\phi(x)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x))
. This gives

\displaystyle(\textrm{\ref{eqn-feynman-calc}})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\Big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)\int\phi(z)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\Big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\frac{1}{2}\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)^{2}\iint\phi(z)^{4}\phi(w)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(w)\Big]+\cdots

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\big]-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\underbrace{\int\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{4}\big]\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)}_{A}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)^{2}\iint\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{4}\phi(w)^{4}\big]\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(w)+\cdots

Treating
\phi(x)
,
\phi(y)
,
\phi(z)
as (jointly Gaussian!) random variables, by (i) of lemma
B.9
and the above rules we write

\displaystyle A
